## Page 1


Liouville Blocks from Spectral Networks
Lotte Hollands and Subrabalan Murugesan
Department of Mathematics, Heriot-Watt University, and
Maxwell Institute for Mathematical Sciences, Edinburgh
Abstract
In this paper, we investigate the role of spectral networks in quantum Liouville
theory, with particular emphasis on spectral networks of Fenchel–Nielsen type. In
the first part, we construct q-parallel transport for Fenchel-Nielsen networks through
q-nonabelianisation, and compare with quantum parallel transport computed using
the Moore–Seiberg formalism. This motivates a proposal for a quantum version of the
NRS proposal. In the second part, we reproduce Liouville conformal blocks through
the standard free-field formalism with Fenchel-Nielsen type integration contours.
However, we observe that this approach is not complete with respect to wall-crossing.
We therefore develop an extension of the free-field formalism to smooth spectral
coverings, with the Maulik Okounkov R-matrix playing a central role. We conjecture
that this new formalism generates the full spectrum of Liouville conformal blocks, and
provides a first-principle definition for Goncharov-Shen conformal blocks.

Introduction
Liouville CFT has played a central role in two-dimensional quantum field theory
for several decades. Beginning with the seminal papers [1, 2], which proposed exact
expressions for its three-point structure constants, Liouville theory emerged as a rare
example of an interacting CFT solvable by analytic bootstrap methods [3, 4]. Subsequent
developments connected Liouville theory to geometry and topology, most notably through
its relation to the quantisation of Teichm¨uller space [5, 6], placing it at the intersection of
conformal field theory, low-dimensional geometry, and quantum topology. More recently,
Liouville theory has been given a rigorous mathematical foundation through probabilistic
methods, culminating in a proof of the DOZZ three-point function [7].
Toda CFTs arise as higher-rank extensions of Liouville theory, in which the single expo-
nential interaction is replaced by a set of interacting fields organised by a Lie algebra. Their
properties have been explored using tools such as the higher-rank conformal bootstrap [8],
the quantisation of moduli spaces of flat G-connections [9], and the AGT correspondence
relating four-dimensional N = 2 QFTs to two-dimensional CFTs [10, 11] (reviewed for
instance in [12]). Despite substantial progress, several foundational questions remain open.
In particular, a universally accepted closed-form expression for three-point functions of ar-
bitrary primary fields is still lacking (but see [13, 14, 15, 16, 17] for various very promising
approaches).
In the mean-time, the notion of a spectral network was introduced in the influential
works [18, 19]. Aside from being a powerful tool to enumerate the various types of BPS
states in four-dimensional N = 2 theories [20, 21, 22, 23], spectral networks were soon
found to be essential for understanding coordinate systems and quantisation in higher
Teichm¨uller theory [24, 25]. See for instance [26] for a thorough and pedagogical review.
In particular, W-abelianisation provides a systematic method to construct systems of
Darboux coordinates on moduli spaces of flat connections, which are known as spectral
coordinates [19, 25]. One of the main advantages of spectral coordinates is their good
WKB properties. This is made precise by the relation of W-abelianisation to the exact WKB
analysis [27]. In particular, this leads to a practical approach of computing the generating
function of oper connections [28, 29], even for higher rank theories [27]. Through the
NS-NRS proposals [30, 31], this generating function is related to the the semi-classical limit
of Toda conformal blocks.
One of the motivations behind this paper is to lift the above relation between spectral
networks and Toda conformal blocks to the quantum level, with the ultimate goal of com-
puting Toda three-point functions with arbitrary insertions. For this, we take inspiration
3


## Page 4


from the free-field approach to Toda CFTs [32, 33], since its screening contours resemble
quantised spectral network trajectories. The relation of the free-field formalism to the
theory of matrix models [34] prompted us to consider free-field correlators with insertions
of screening charges along the spectral network trajectories. A similar, but novel, strategy
that we refer to as CFT-nonabelianisation was developed in [35] for Liouville theory at
c = 1. In contrast to the standard approach of considering a singular spectral curve in the
large-N limit, CFT-nonabelianisation sets out with a spectral network on a smooth spectral
curve. In this paper, we compare both approaches, before arguing that the framework of
[35] is ultimately the right one, and we initiate its extension to the case c ̸= 1 for general
Toda theories.
W-abelianisation generates various types of spectral coordinates, including the cel-
ebrated Fock-Goncharov cluster coordinates [9, 24], but it is well-known that Fenchel-
Nielsen type spectral coordinates play an elevated role in the context of Toda CFTs. In
particular, only when expressed in terms of complexified Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates,
the generating function of oper connections reproduces the Yang-Yang function [31]. Yet,
it was found more recently that the perspective of cluster coordinates provides a more
complete and unifying picture. Indeed, the generating function of oper connections in
terms of cluster coordinates defines a non-perturbative completion (in the sense of [36])
of the Yang-Yang function. From the perspective of the four-dimensional N = 2 theory,
the Yang-Yang function computes the four-dimensional instanton partition function in the
Nekrasov-Shatashvili limit [30]; in addition, the non-perturbative Yang-Yang function en-
codes the complete four-dimensional BPS particle spectrum [29, 37]. At present, however,
the role of generic cluster coordinates in Toda CFTs is not very well-understood (see [38]
for some progress in this direction). This paper also attempts to (partially) remedy this.
The main results in this paper are as follows:
• In §2, we construct SL(2, C) monodromy representations in terms of complexified
Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates for arbitrary Riemann surfaces C (with regular punc-
tures), by computing parallel transport matrices via W-abelianisation on the gauged
three-punctured sphere.
• In §3, we bring the Moore-Seiberg matrices [39], computing quantum SL(2, C) paral-
lel transport on any surface C (with regular punctures), in the same W-abelianised
gauge. This shows a simple relation between quantum and semi-classical parallel
transports, and suggests a quantisation of the complexified Fenchel-Nielsen coordi-
nates, when acting on conformal blocks, in terms of difference operators (as already
known from [40, 41]).
• Furthermore, in §3, we work out the NRS proposal in the form of [29] for the gauged
3-punctured sphere, and formulate a quantum NRS proposal. The quantum NRS
proposal says that the quantum Fenchel-Nielsen twist coordinates, when acting on
Liouville conformal blocks, compute the derivatives of the Yang-Yang function. (The
action of the quantum spectral coordinates on Liouville blocks is defined in the sense
of CFT-abelianisation, as discussed in full detail in §5.)
• Finally, in §3, we argue that Fenchel-Nielsen type spectral networks may be inter-
preted as symmetry defects (as in [42]) in Liouville theory.
4


## Page 5


• An alternative approach for computing quantum parallel transports in terms of
any system of quantum spectral coordinates (with the emphasis on quantum clus-
ter coordinates) was developed in [43], and called q-nonabelianisation. In §4, we
formulate q-nonabelianisation for Fenchel-Nielsen type spectral networks and com-
pute q-parallel transport on arbitrary surfaces C (with regular punctures). We find
agreement with Moore-Seiberg parallel transport up to (somewhat trivial) winding
factors.
• In §5, we compute standard free-field correlators in Liouville theory with respect
to Fenchel-Nielsen type spectral networks. In the large-N limit, we find precise
agreement with Liouville conformal blocks (in a series expansion in the complex
structure parameters on C), although this requires a somewhat ad-hoc argument to
implement arbitrary wall-crossings.
• For a more conceptual approach, we propose an extension of the CFT-abelianisation
formalism [35] to arbitrary central charge and arbitrary rank, in which a key role
is played by the Maulik-Okounkov R-matrix [44]. We argue that the resulting cor-
relators can be interpreted as non-perturbative partition functions, in the sense of
[36], and that they compute Liouville conformal blocks (i.e. Nekrasov partition func-
tions) for Fenchel-Nielsen type spectral networks, while providing a definition for
Goncharov-Shen blocks [38] for Fock-Goncharov type spectral networks.
1.1
Outline
The outline of this paper is as follows1:
Let C be a (possibly punctured) Riemann surface. Section 2 starts with a quick refresher
on rank 2 spectral networks Wϑ(ϕ2), which are defined in terms of a quadratic differen-
tial ϕ2 together with a phase ϑ. In particular, we introduce the class of Fock-Goncharov
type spectral networks WFG, which are dual to ideal triangulations of C, and the class of
Fenchel-Nielsen type spectral networks WFN, which are dual to a pants decomposition of
C and can be built out of copies of the so-called Fenchel-Nielsen molecules.
We then review the W-abelianisation method, which brings any SL(2, C) flat con-
nection ∇on C in an almost diagonal form with respect to the network W, and the
W-nonabelianisation method, which computes the non-abelian parallel transport of
an SL(2, C) flat connection ∇in terms of the abelian parallel transport of an SL(2, C)-
equivariant connection ∇ab on the covering Σ, defined by ϕ2. W-nonabelianisation with
respect to a Fock-Goncharov type spectral network yields monodromy representations in
terms of Fock-Goncharov cluster coordinates, while W-nonabelianisation with respect to
a Fenchel-Nielsen type spectral network yields monodromy representations in terms of
complexified Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates.
We emphasise that both the W-abelianisation as well as the W-nonabelianisation
methods commute with the operation of gluing pairs of pants when the network W is of

independently from the rest of the paper.

Our last result in section 3 identifies Fenchel-Nielsen spectral networks as symmetry
defects in the Liouville CFT. Indeed, since the Moore-Seiberg parallel transport on any
surface C can be brought in W-abelianised gauge with respect to a Fenchel-Nielsen spectral
network, the spectral network itself acts on degenerate operators in the CFT as a symmetry
defect.
In section 4, we propose yet another quantisation of SL(2, C)-parallel transports on the
surface C using skein theory and q-nonabelianisation. We start with an introduction to

Physically, we remind ourselves in section 4.1.3 that q-nonabelianisation is a version of
a UV-IR map between an SL(2, C)-Chern-Simons theory on C × I and a GL(1, C)-Chern-
Simons theory on Σ × I. Equivalently, this can also be phrased as a UV-IR map between
a boundary Liouville theory on C and a boundary free boson theory on Σ (we make
8


## Page 9


this precise through the CFT-abelianisation map in section 5.4.). Then, the quantum Y-
coordinates can be realised as open abelian Verlinde line operators in a free boson theory on
Σ (equivalently, as non-compact Wilson lines (labelled by the fundamental representation)
in a GL(1, C)-Chern-Simons theory on Σ × I).
The physical interpretation of q-nonabelianisation allows us to evaluate the quantum Y-
coordinates on the gauged 3-sphere in terms of Heisenberg conformal blocks on the cover Σ
(see section 4.3.1). We find that all the quantum coordinates must act multiplicatively on
these Heisenberg conformal blocks, where the multiplicative constant is the semi-classical
spectral coordinate. Moreover, assuming that q-nonabelianisation and CFT-abelianisation
commute, we also deduce the abelianisation of the space of Liouville conformal blocks on
the gauged 3-sphere with one degenerate insertion.
We extend this construction to arbitrary surfaces C in section 4.4. We argue that it is
sufficient to compute q-parallel transports restricted to individual pairs of pants in a pants
decomposition of C, and glue the resulting q-parallel transports by inserting the matrix
(see eq. (4.95))

Our aim in section 5 is to construct general Liouville conformal blocks from Fenchel-
Nielsen type spectral networks through the free-field formalism. Motivated by the relation
between the free-field formalism and Penner-type matrix models (reviewed in section 5.2),
we define free-field correlators supplemented with non-local screening charges on the
trajectories of a degenerate Fenchel-Nielsen spectral network. Through examples (on the
3 and 4-punctured spheres) we show that these correlators indeed reproduce Liouville
conformal blocks. In particular, we reproduce the square-root of the Liouville 3-point
function at the Fenchel-Nielsen phase (see eq. (5.114))

1.2
Outlook
We conclude by outlining several open problems and directions for future research:
• In §4 we have given a description of q-parallel transport in terms of quantum Fenchel-
Nielsen coordinates, through the q-nonabelianisation approach of Neitzke and Yan in
[43]. In particular, we found agreement with the Moore-Seiberg approach in Liouville
CFT. Yet another approach for computing quantum parallel transport is given by
Schrader and Shapiro in [45]. It would be worthwhile to analyse the similarities
and differences between both approaches, and compare computations in terms of
Fock-Goncharov as well as Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates.
• In section 3.3.3, we propose a quantisation of the Nekrasov-Rosly-Shatashvili pro-
posal from [31] (in the language of [29]). The verification of this proposal relies on a
detailed formulation of the CFT-abelianisation map. With the progress made in the
last section of this paper, it is possible to work out the quantum NRS proposal in ex-
amples. In particular, it is tempting to think that this proposal leads to (a refinement
of) the Baxter TQ-equation (see for instance [46, eq. 7.11]) on the 4-punctured sphere
for the pure SU(2) theory.
11


## Page 12


• Originally in [43], the q-nonabelianisation map was only defined for generic Fock-
Goncharov networks. Although we are able to define q-parallel transports for
Fenchel-Nielsen networks, we have not addressed the q-nonabelianisation of ar-
bitrary closed links (with respect to a Fenchel-Nielsen network) in this paper. It
is now known [47] that q-nonabelianisation for Fock-Goncharov networks agrees
with other triangulation-dependent quantisation procedures in the literature. The
final piece of this puzzle is to extend the q-nonabelianisation map to Fenchel-Nielsen
networks, and compare it with other pants decomposition-dependent quantisation
procedures such as the one described in [48].
• The CFT-nonabelianisation formalism proposed in §5.4.3 requires significant fur-
ther development. In particular, it is imperative to computationally verify that
the proposed Heisenberg conformal blocks on Σ with respect to Fenchel-Nielsen
type spectral networks indeed compute Liouville conformal blocks on C. Another
pressing matter is to derive the q-nonabelianisation map from the perspective of the
CFT-nonabelianisation formalism.
• Unitarity in Liouville theory requires b > 0 (see the discussion around eq. (3.10))
and thus β < 0. However, at several points in the matrix model analysis in section 5
we prefer β > 0 over β < 0. Even more strongly, the global free-field picture in
§5.4.3, which places screening charges of either type along the ij-trajectories of a
spectral network, is only well-defined for β > 0. See remark 5.6 for more details. It is
desirable to clarify this.
• Of course, we would like to be able to generalise all results in this paper to higher
rank. In fact, one of the original motivations that initiated this project was to find
the relation between the higher rank Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates introduced from a
spectral network perspective by Hollands and Neitzke in [27], and from a CFT free-
field perspective by Coman, Pomon and Teschner in [16]. The latter article prompted
us to study the free-field formalism and to investigate its relation to W-abelianisation.
Our current perspective on this is that the two systems of coordinates developed
in the papers above are not related in a simple way, but that it is possible to find a
quantisation of the higher Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates described in [27] through the
free-field formalism.
Indeed, the free-field formalism, as well as its description in terms of β-deformed
matrix models, has a well-known generalisation to higher rank [49, 50]. We conjecture
that Toda conformal blocks can be computed as WK free-field correlators in the large
N limit, whose contour system is chosen to align with a suitable degenerate network
of higher Fenchel-Nielsen type. For the T3 theory we should choose the (massive)
circular Fenchel-Nielsen network in [22] in the degenerate limit in which branch-
points of the same kind come together. Note that the degenerate limit is the opposite
of the massless limit, and that the differentials (ϕ2, ϕ3) lose their dependence on
the Coulomb parameter u in this limit. Even better would be to construct these
Toda conformal blocks through the global free-field formalism generalised for WK-
algebras.

2
Gauging and gluing Fenchel-Nielsen molecules
This section is mostly a synopsis of material in [18, 19, 25, 28, 27, 29] that will be
built upon in later sections. We start this section in §2.1 with a quick intro to spectral
networks W on a (possibly punctured) Riemann surface C, with special emphasis on those
of Fenchel-Nielsen type. In §2.2 we summarise the abelianisation and non-abelianisation
methods that bring the parallel transport of flat SL(2, C)-connections on C in an almost-
diagonal gauge with respect to a fixed spectral network W. We emphasise that both
methods commute with the operation of gluing pairs of pants when the network W is
of Fenchel-Nielsen type. In §2.3 we derive nonabelian parallel transport matrices on the
gauged 3-punctured sphere in terms of Fenchel-Nielsen length-twist coordinates, while in
§2.4 we discuss how to glue gauged 3-punctured spheres to compute parallel transport on
arbitrary Riemann surfaces (possibly with regular punctures) in terms of Fenchel-Nielsen
length-twist coordinates.
2.1
Basics of spectral networks
Consider a degree 2 spectral cover Σ ⊂T∗C of a (possibly punctured) Riemann
surface C cut out by an equation of the type3
Σ :
λ2 + ϕ2 = 0,
(2.1)
where ϕ2 is a quadratic differential on C and λ is the tautological 1-form on T∗C. Such
a spectral covering is in general branched, and for the moment we will assume that the
ramification points are simple. We shall choose a system of branch-cuts on the base curve C
3Note that ϕ2 is defined here with an additional sign compared to (for instance) [18, 25], yet agrees with
the conventions of (for instance) [19] and Andy Neitzke’s swn plotter [51].
13


## Page 14


to trivialise the covering. All final results are independent of the choice of trivialisation,
however, and can be presented in a trivialisation-independent way.
Spectral network
Let λi denote the restriction of λ to the i-th sheet of Σ. Choose a phase
ϑ ∈R/2πZ. We define an ij-trajectory on C to be a (real) curve w(t) : R →C such that
locally, for each point z = w(t) on the curve,
e−iϑ(λi −λj)(w′(t)) ∈R≥0.
(2.2)
It is easy to check that every simple branch-point of type (ij) emits three trajectories, which
are either of type ij or ji. The set of all ij−trajectories defines a foliation of the surface C.
This is called the WKB foliation.
Remark 2.1. Leaves of the WKB foliation may also be defined as (real) curves w(t) : R →C
such that

 λi −λj
 = constant.
(2.3)
Since λi = −λj, all trajectories are flow lines for the Hamiltonian function Im(e−iϑ√ϕ2).
Equivalently, 12-trajectories (21-trajectories) are steepest ascent (descent) trajectories for
the Morse function Re(e−iϑ√ϕ2). This will become important in §5 in the context of matrix
models.
An ij-trajectory is said to be critical if one of its end-points lies on a branch-point.
Critical trajectories are sometimes called walls as well. We orient critical trajectories away
from the branch-point. The union of all critical trajectories is called the critical graph. The
critical graph together with a choice of branch-cuts, orientations and labelings is called a
spectral network Wϑ(ϕ2) (or more precisely, a WKB spectral network [18]). See fig. 1 for an
example. We will refer to the connected components of C\W as cells.
Degenerate networks
We will also be interested in the degenerate limit in which ϕ2
becomes the square of a 1-form ω on C. In this case, the spectral cover
Σ :
λ2 + ω2 = 0
(2.4)
degenerates into two copies of the curve C that are glued transversely at the zeroes of ω.
In particular, this implies that the ramification points of the covering Σ →C are no longer
simple, and now emit four trajectories. We shall refer to the resulting spectral network,
defined by ϕsing
2
= ω2, as a degenerate network Wϑ(ϕsing
2
). See fig. 5 for an example of a
degenerate network of Fenchel-Nielsen type (explained next).
Fock-Goncharov and Fenchel-Nielsen
Critical trajectories generically end at a puncture,
but may also run between two branch-points. Such trajectories are called saddle trajectories
or double walls. When a network has no saddle trajectories, the network is said to be of
Fock-Goncharov type (FG-type) [24]. An FG network is dual to an ideal triangulation of the
base curve C. (That is, the edges of the triangulation do not intersect the trajectories of the
spectral network, but instead run parallel to them.) See fig. 1.

On the other hand, for certain phases, all ij−trajectories (critical and non-critical) may
become compact curves on C. When the network is dual to a pants decomposition of C,
we instead call it of Fenchel-Nielsen type (FN-type) [25]. See fig. 2 and 3 for examples. The
appearance of pants decompositions is the first indication that spectral networks play an
important role in the description of conformal field theories such as Liouville theory.
Let us for the moment restrict ourselves to surfaces C with regular punctures.4 Suppose
that we fix a pants decomposition P of the surface C, with pants-cycles ap and loops ak
around the punctures zk. (A pair of pants in P may thus have up to three regular punctures
as its boundary components.) Given any collection of positive numbers ap and µk, we can
find a differential ϕ2 and a phase ϑFN such that [52]

Gluing Fenchel-Nielsen molecules
The restriction of any FN network WFN to a pair of
pants in P is isotopic to one of the two FN molecules. This operation is somewhat subtle
though: the restriction of the spectral network WϑFN±η, for finite η > 0, to a pair of pants
is not isotopic to a spectral network on the 3-punctured sphere obtained by changing the
phase of a FN molecule by finite η > 0. Let us explain this in more detail.
The resulting network on the 3-punctured sphere consists of just 6 trajectories that start
at one of the two branch-points and wind into one of the three punctures. (This network is
dual to an ideal triangulation with two triangles.) Instead, the trajectories of the network
WϑFN±η on generic C, which originate at the branch-points of a given pair of pants in P,
will either wind into a puncture or cross a pants tube. This implies that the restriction of the

spectral network WϑFN±η to any pair of pants will have additional incoming trajectories
(compared to the network on the 3-punctured sphere) from each boundary component
that is not a puncture. See 6 for an example on the 4-punctured sphere.
Now, when we tune η →0, the trajectories of WϑFN±η start to wind more and more
frequently around the punctures and pants tubes. Precisely at the critical phase ϑFN all
trajectories are compact, so that no trajectory traverses any pants tube any longer. The
restriction of the critical network WFN to the pair of pants therefore does have the same
topology as that of an FN molecule. As a consequence, we can simply glue FN molecules
on pairs of pants to obtain FN networks on generic surfaces C.
In the following, we will mostly consider FN networks that are built out of copies of
molecule I. This is mostly for presentation purposes, since molecule I does not distinguish
between punctures. We expect that the main results hold for any other configuration of
molecules as well.

Higher rank spectral networks
In this paper we mostly focus on spectral networks of
rank 2. Yet, we believe most of the discussions can be extended to higher rank spectral
networks, and they do come up explicitly in §5.4. We therefore introduce them briefly
here.
Spectral networks of type AN−1 are defined with respect to a degree N spectral cover
Σ ⊂T∗C cut out by an equation of the type [19]
Σ :
λN + ϕ2λN−2 + . . . + ϕN ,
(2.8)
where ϕ = (ϕ2, . . . , ϕN) is a tuple of k-differentials on C and λ is the tautological 1-form
on T∗C. As previously, the family of spectral networks Wϑ(ϕ), for ϑ ∈R/2πZ, is defined
as the collection of critical ij-trajectories, oriented away from the branch-points.
2.2
Recap of abelianisation and nonabelianisation
Spectral networks encode a lot of information, for instance about the BPS spectrum
of N = 2 theories of class S [18, 20, 19, 22]. But they can also be employed to construct
Darboux coordinates on moduli spaces of flat connections on C, through a procedure
that is called W-abelianisation [19, 25]. We briefly summarise W-abelianisation here for
spectral networks W = Wϑ(ϕ2) of rank 2.
Note that the following discussion is valid only for non-degenerate spectral networks.
To be precise, W-abelianisation is not even defined at any value ϑ for which the network W

trajectories into the left (right) pair of pants.

has saddle trajectories. If the spectral network does have saddles, we define its W-
abelianisation as the average of the W-abelianisations of its two resolutions. We will go
through explicit examples of this type in §2.3.
2.2.1
W-abelianisation
Fix a rank 2 vector bundle E →C and consider any flat SL(2, C)-connection ∇on E
together with a W-framing. The W-framing is a discrete choice on ∇that depends only
on the isotopy class of W. Its purpose is to render the W-abelianisation procedure (as
explained below) 1-1. If W is a spectral network of FG type, the W-framing consists
of a choice of eigenline ℓk at each puncture. If W is a spectral network of FN type, the
W-framing consists of a choice of eigenline ℓ±
n in each direction of going around a ring
domain [25, §5.3]. We require ℓ+
n ̸= ℓ−
n , so that the W-framing for an FN network only
exists when the monodromy around each ring domain is diagonalisable.
The task in W-abelianisation is to bring ∇into an almost-diagonal form. More precisely,
we want to find a gauge in which ∇is diagonal in each cell of C\W and in which the
diagonal bases across ij-trajectories are related by unipotent transformations. That is, we
look for a basis (s1, s2) of local sections of E in each cell of C\W such that
∇si = di ⊗si
(2.9)
for some di ∈T∗C, and such that two local bases across an ij-trajectory are related by the
transformation

(2.10)
We also impose that the local sections are shuffled as
si 7→sj
sj 7→−si,
(2.11)
when crossing a branch-cut labeled by (ij). With the above choice of W-framing for
spectral networks of FG and FN type, we can find a unique almost-diagonal form of this
kind for every W-framed flat connection ∇(up to SL(2, C)-equivalence) [25].
Given the almost-diagonal form of ∇, we can define a C∗-connection ∇ab on the cover Σ
through the equation
∇absi = di ⊗si.
(2.12)
The abelian connection ∇ab is indeed well-defined globally precisely because the gauge
transformations between the cells can be brought into unipotent form. It is almost-flat, in
the sense that its holonomy around the lift of a branch-point is −1 instead of 1.
The resulting procedure defines the W-abelianisation map

Consider, for example, an open path p on C that crosses an ij-trajectory of the spectral
network W. Then the collection of lifts of p to the cover contains the two canonical lifts of
p to each sheet of the covering, i.e. the direct lifts, as well as precisely one detour path that
runs from sheet i to sheet j along the ij-trajectory, as illustrated in fig. 8. That is, suppose
that the path p crosses the ij−trajectory at a point w ∈C. Then the detour path starts on
sheet i until it hits the ij−trajectory, when it follows the trajectory from the point w(i) on
sheet i to the point w(j) on sheet j, encircles the branch-point, and then continues on sheet j.
Note that the sheet labelings at the start and end of the detour path are chosen in such a
way that the abelian parallel transport aij along the detour path δij is exponentially small in
the WKB approximation (2.22). Indeed, since e−iϑ(λi −λj) ≥0 along the ij-trajectory, the
abelian holonomy aij is exponentially small in the limit ϵ →0 with the above conventions.
6Let us emphasize that W-nonabelianisation is the inverse of W-abelianisation for those types of spectral
networks W for which the latter map is 1-1. This is true for rank 2 spectral networks of Fenchel-Nielsen or
Fock-Goncharov type [25], but not true in general (see [27] for an example). In general, W-abelianisation
and W-nonabelianisation should be treated as self-standing procedures.
23


## Page 24


The non-abelian parallel transport Γ(p) of the flat connection ∇along the base path p
is then simply the sum of the abelian parallel transports of the almost-flat connection ∇ab
along the collection of lifts to Σ. In particular, note that the monodromy along a closed
path on C can receive contributions from open paths on the cover Σ.
2.3
Gauging the 3-punctured sphere
As we have explained above, an FN network is isotopic to one of the two FN molecules
when it is restricted to a pair of pants. Yet, the moduli space of flat connections restricted to
any pair of pants (when it is embedded in a larger surface C and thus contains fewer than
three regular punctures as its boundary components) is different from the moduli space
of flat connections on the 3-punctured sphere (where we fix the conjugacy class of the
monodromies around the punctures). Indeed, the complex dimension of the moduli spaces
of flat connections of the larger Riemann surface C increases by two, and not just one, for
every additional internal pants cycle. Whereas the conjugacy class of the monodromy
around an internal pant cycle parametrises one additional complex degree of freedom,
each internal pants cycle is associated with an additional complex degree of freedom.
We can replicate these additional degrees of freedom on the pair of pants by cutting out
a small open disc around each puncture of the 3-punctured sphere and fixing a trivialisation
of the rank 2 bundle E at a marked point on each new boundary component. We refer
to the above operation as gauging the puncture.7 From the abelianisation perspective,
gauging a puncture on C introduces two additional spectral coordinates on C: the abelian
holonomy (which we refer to as Mk) along a lift Ak of the loop ak around the puncture, and
the abelian holonomy (which we refer to as Tk) along a dual open path Bk on the cover Σ
that connects the two lifts of the marked point. This is illustrated in fig. 9. To be precise,
we choose the lift Ak such that |Mk| < 1 and the lift Bk such that the intersection product
Ak · Bk = 1. This implies that log Mk is a complexified FN length coordinate. Similarly,
log Tk is a complexified FN twist coordinate [25, §8.4].
Note that we can phrase the choice of FN length coordinate in terms of the W-framing
as follows. If the W-framing is chosen such that the small eigenvalue |Mk| < 1 (versus
the large eigenvalue |Mk| > 1) is assigned to the orientation of the loop ak, and if the
orientation of ak agrees with the orientation of the ij-trajectories in this ring domain, then
we define the exponentiated FN length coordinate as the abelian holonomy along the
lift Ak of the loop ak to the jth sheet (versus the ith sheet).
Vice versa, if we instead decide to fix the lift Ak once and for all, we have to specify
the W-framing such that |Mk| < 1. This choice of W-framing is called the WKB framing
in [29], since in the setting of ϵ-connections and the WKB approximation (2.22), it implies
that the exponentiated FN length coordinate is exponentially small in the limit ϵ →0.
We name the object that is obtained after gauging all three punctures of the 3-punctured
sphere a gauged 3-punctured sphere, or gauged 3-sphere in short. Note that the gauged
3-sphere is equivalent to the gauged pair of pants. If the conjugation class of the monodromy
7The nomenclature ”gauging” originates from the AGT correspondence [41], in which vertex operators
Vα correspond to flavour symmetries in the dual N = 2 theory, and where summing over the complete basis
of descendants ∑Y LYVα corresponds to gauging this flavour symmetry (see [53] for more details).
24


## Page 25


Figure 9: When gauging a puncture we cut out a small open disc around the puncture and
fix the trivialisation of the bundle E at a marked point on the boundary of the open disc.
As a result, the abelian holonomies along the paths Ak and Bk on Σ turn into new spectral
coordinates.
along one boundary component (of a gauged 3-sphere) is the same as along another bound-
ary component (of possibly a different gauged 3-sphere), these boundary components can
be glued by identifying the fixed trivialisations at their marked points. Gluing of gauged
3-spheres can thus be defined on the level of moduli spaces of flat SL(2, C)-connections.
Moreover, it can be shown that this gluing operation commutes with W-abelianisation
for spectral networks of FN type [25]. The reason why this works is that none of the
trajectories of the FN network cross the new pants cycle. Abelianisation thus brings the
monodromies along the new pants cycle into diagonal form.
Nonabelianising Fenchel-Nielsen molecules
In order to make concrete the above discus-
sion, we apply the nonabelianisation method (as formulated in [25, §7]8) to FN molecule I
(as illustrated in fig. 10) on the gauged 3-punctured sphere.
Consider the path groupoid in fig. 10 relative to molecule I, where we fix a trivialisation
of the bundle E at the three marked points (in dark green) to the left of the punctures. This
implies that the abelian connection on the covering Σ is completely fixed in terms of six
spectral coordinates: the eigenvalues M1,2,3 of the abelian holonomy around the punctures,
and the abelian parallel transport coefficients Ta,b,c along lifts of the paths
pa = p1 p2 p3,
pb = p4 p5 p6,
pc = p7 p8 p9,
(2.25)
to the covering Σ (starting from sheet 2 and ending on sheet 1).
As we mentioned previously, the nonabelianisation method cannot be applied to FN
type networks precisely at the critical phase. Therefore, the best we can do is apply the
nonabelianisation method to their resolutions and take an average. The families of 12
and 21-trajectories in the resolutions are then encoded in the so-called S-matrices, which
describe the unipotent gauge transformations across the saddle trajectories in the given
resolution.
Since we want to relate the resulting SL(2, C)-connections for both resolutions, rather

2.4
Gluing through nonabelianisation
Also, W-nonabelianisation for an FN network WFN (defined with respect to a pants
decomposition P) commutes with dissecting the surface C into pairs of pants (in the pants
decomposition P). Just as before, this W-nonabelianisation is defined as the average of
both resolutions W±
FN.
The reader might be concerned about what happens to the additional incoming trajec-
tories into each pair of pants at phase ϑFN ± η, with η > 0 finite, in this process. (Recall
that fig. 6 illustrates such trajectories in the 4-punctured sphere example.) It turns out that
these additional trajectories decouple in the limit η →0+, so that the S-matrices for the
resolutions W±
FN reproduce the S-matrices on the gauged 3-sphere, computed in §2.3. We
have included the precise argument in Appendix A.
As a concrete example, we compute the nonabelian parallel transport matrices on the
4-punctured sphere:
2.4.1
Example: 4-punctured sphere
Consider the 4-punctured sphere together with the FN-type spectral network in fig. 3.
In the following we detail how to construct the monodromy representation on this sphere
by gluing two gauged 3-spheres and then ”ungauging” the 4 remaining punctures. Note
that the moduli space of flat SL(2, C)-connections on the gauged 4-punctured sphere is
complex 10-dimensional, whereas the moduli space of flat SL(2, C)-connections on the
(ungauged) 4-punctured sphere is complex 2-dimensional.
Consider the path groupoid as illustrated in fig. 12. Nonabelianisation with respect to an
FN network implies that the resulting SL(2, C)-monodromies around the four punctures zk,
as well as the pants-curve a, are diagonal. We introduce parameters Mk and A such that
these monodromies equal

3
Liouville CFT and quantum Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates
The goal of this section is to describe a quantisation of the Fenchel–Nielsen parallel
transports derived in the previous section. We achieve this using the Moore-Seiberg
transformations of conformal blocks in the framework of Liouville conformal field theory.
By studying the transformation properties of the BPZ connection for Liouville theory, we
are able to show that the Moore-Seiberg transformations quantise the Fenchel-Nielsen
parallel transports.
In §3.1, we provide the necessary background by introducing the Lax connection,
conformal blocks, (degenerate) vertex operators, and the BPZ connection. In §3.2, we sum-
marise how to compute quantum parallel transports using the Moore-Seiberg groupoid,
and how to bring the Moore-Seiberg (MS) parallel transport in the Fenchel-Nielsen gauge,
relative to a chosen pants decomposition. In all this, we emphasize that results on the
gauged three-punctured sphere can easily be generalised to an arbitrary (punctured) Rie-
mann surface through a gluing construction. We furthermore show how the semi-classical
conformal blocks are encoded in the evaluation of the Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates on the
33


## Page 34


Lax connection, and how the Moore-Seiberg monodromies quantise this result. Finally,
in §3.4, we observe that these results lead to an interpretation of Fenchel-Nielsen type
spectral networks as topological defects in Liouville theory.
3.1
Basics of Liouville CFT
Liouville theory is a quantum field theory described by a scalar field φ(z, ¯z) on a
Riemann surface C with an exponential potential. It is governed by the (renormalised)
action [54],

In the semi-classical limit b →0, the equations of motion of the Liouville theory

At the quantum level, Liouville theory is a two-dimensional conformal field theory,

The basic local operators in Liouville theory are the primary vertex operators V∆α(z, z)

∆α = α(Q −α),

3.1.1
Conformal blocks
The correlation functions of Liouville theory factorise into holomorphic (chiral) and
anti-holomorphic (anti-chiral) contributions. More precisely, given a pair of pants decom-
position of the surface C, and given that the OPE between two primary fields necessarily
has the form
Vα1(z1, ¯z1)Vα2(z2, ¯z2) =
Z
dα C(α1, α2, α∗) |z1 −z2|2(∆α−∆1−∆2) (V∆α(z1, ¯z1) + O(|z1 −z2|)) ,
(3.15)
where α∗= Q −α is the conjugate momentum, and where the subleading terms are
corrections from descendant fields, it follows that any Liouville correlation function can be
written as an integral over internal momenta through the pants cycles, with the integrand
consisting of a product of three-point functions C, one for each pair of pants, times a
factorised product FF over descendant contributions.
The factors F and F are known as holomorphic and anti-holomorphic conformal blocks,
respectively. Whereas the three-point functions C encode the dynamics of Liouville theory,
the conformal blocks F and F are entirely determined by the Virasoro algebra.
For example, the four-point correlation function of primary vertex operators may be
written as

In the following, we restrict ourselves to holomorphic Virasoro blocks F Vir, and drop

genus zero surfaces and extended to higher genus surfaces through a procedure known as
gluing. We denote the space of conformal blocks on a surface C by CB(C).

describe how the conformal blocks transform under infinitesimal conformal symmetries.

The space of conformal blocks on an arbitrary Riemann surface C is, similarly, infinite-
dimensional in general. Furthermore, we can also define the space of gauged conformal
blocks gCB(C) on any surface C by summing over all possible vertex operator representa-
tions at its punctures.
As we vary the complex structure on C, the space of conformal blocks organises itself
into a holomorphic vector bundle CB over the moduli space M of complex structures on
C. The variation of the conformal block in the direction of a Beltrami differential µ can be
expressed in terms of a projectively flat (or twisted) connection on M, whose connection
1-form is defined by inserting

this choice by definition (since any two-dimensional CFT ought to be modular invariant).

3.1.2
Null-vector decoupling
Generically, a Verma module Vα forms an irreducible representation of the Virasoro
algebra. However, for special values
αp,q = (1 −p) 1
2b + (1 −q) b
2
with p, q ∈Z+,
(3.35)
of the Liouville momentum, the corresponding Virasoro module is reducible and contains
a sub-module generated by a null state (or null vector). All states in this sub-module
have vanishing norm, and may thus be consistently quotiented out. The resulting finite-
dimensional module Vp,q is known as a degenerate representation, the associated primary
vertex operator Vp,q := Vαp,q is known as a degenerate primray vertex operator, and the
corresponding momentum αp,q, as a degenerate momentum.15
The degenerate momenta αp,q are closely related to the fundamental weights of SL(2, C).
Indeed, we may rewrite eq. (3.35) as
αp,q = −1
b ωp−1 −b ωq−1,
(3.36)
where ωp−1 and ωq−1 are the highest weights of the pth and qth symmetric tensor repre-
sentations of SL(2, C), respectively. In this sense, a degenerate operator with momentum
α1,q quantises the q-dimensional representation of SL(2, C). This can be made more precise
through skein theory (see §4.1.3). For quantum monodromy representations, the degener-
ate operator V1,2 corresponding to the 2-dimensional or defining representation of SL(2, C)
is the most relevant to us.
If a degenerate vertex operator Vp,q(z) is inserted in a conformal block F Vir(. . .), the
algebraic null-vector constraint turns into a differential operator of degree pq acting on
the block F Vir(Vp,q(z), . . .). The resulting differential equation is known as the null-vector
decoupling equation or the BPZ equation [59]. For instance, for the degenerate vertex
operator V1,2(z) we obtain the degree 2 differential equation16
(∂2
z + b2T(z))F Vir(V1,2(z), . . .) = 0.
(3.37)
The space of conformal blocks F Vir(Vp,q(z), . . .) may thus be identified with the space of
solutions to the BPZ equation, and is therefore pq-dimensional.
The rank pq matches with the number of fusion channels in the OPE between the
degenerate vertex operator Vp,q(z) and another generic vertex operator [Vα] ∈Vα,

Note that the OPE (3.38) also implies constraints on the internal momenta ⃗a of the
conformal block F Vir, which are generically unrestricted. For instance, the three-point

Comparing the BPZ equation (3.37) with the Lax equation eq. (3.5) suggests that the
BPZ equation for V1,2 should be viewed as a quantisation of the Lax equation. This implies
that the degenerate vertex operator V1,2(z) should be viewed as the quantisation of the
semi-classical field ψ(z). More precisely, the BPZ equation (3.37) should be interpreted
as a ”quantum oper” connection on a rank 2 vector bundle over C, associated with the
fundamental representation of SL(2). We clarify what we mean by this in §3.2, where
we discuss the global transformation properties of the BPZ equation (3.37) on a general
Riemann surface.
More generally, the BPZ equation for a degenerate operator V1,p(z) should be thought
of as a quantum connection in a rank p vector bundle, associated with the pth symmetric
tensor representation of SL(2), whereas the BPZ equation for Vp,q(z) is associated with a
component of the tensor product of the pth and the qth symmetric tensor representations
of SL(2).
3.1.3

AGT correspondence states that the supersymmetric partition function Z[S4

The semi-clasical limit of the AGT correspondence can be understood via the Nekrasov-
Rosly-Shatashvili (NRS) proposal [31], which states that the generating function of the
family of opers, when expressed in terms of complex FN-type coordinates, computes the
effective twisted superpotential eWeff for the associated class S theory T[C].
The easiest way to understand this proposal is to consider the theory T[C] on a back-
ground R2 × Dϵ where Dϵ is the cigar geometry with an Ω−deformation.17 At low
energies, we can reduce the theory along Dϵ to an effective 2d N = (2, 2) theory of twisted
chiral superfields (sourced by the 4d vector multiplets) on R2, coupled to an effective
twisted superpotential eWeff. In [30], Nekrasov and Shatashvili proposed that the effective
twisted superpotential and the instanton partition function for the theory T[C] are related
as
eWeff(ϵ) = lim
ϵ2→0 ϵ2 log Z[R4
ϵ1=ϵ, ϵ2].
(3.43)
Furthermore, they argued that the effective twisted superpotential plays the role of a Yang-
Yang functional in a quantum integrable system underlying the Seiberg-Witten geometry
of T[C]. This is now known as the Bethe-gauge correspondence.
Through the AGT-correspondence for general C, the exponent of the one-loop con-
tribution to eWeff is closely related to the square-root of the three-point contribution to
the associated Liouville correlator (see the discussion around eq. (3.93) and (3.96) for the
17Starting from the Ω−background R2

and then replacing the standard metric on R2

42


## Page 43


precise statement), and the exponent of the instanton contribution to eWeff is equal to a
corresponding conformal block F, all in the limit ϵ2 ∼b →0. This establishes that the
semi-classical (b →0) limit of Liouville conformal blocks is captured by the Yang-Yang
function of a quantum integrable system determined by T[C].
At the same time, it was argued by Nekrasov, Rosly and Shatashvili in [31] that the
superpotential eWeff computes the difference of two generating functions of holomorphic
Lagrangian submanifolds inside the moduli space of flat ϵ-connections. This can be seen
by, instead of reducing the 4d theory on all of Dϵ to get a 2d theory, compactifying along
the circle direction of the cigar (i.e. write Dϵ = R+ × S1 where the S1 degenerates at the
tip of the cigar) to obtain a 3d N = 4 theory on R2 × R+. Then, the two holomorphic
Lagrangian submanifolds correspond to a choice of two 1
2-BPS boundary conditions at the
two ends of R+ [62].
The boundary condition at the tip of R+ is purely dictated by the geometry, and
is described by the Lagrangian submanifold of ϵ-opers inside the moduli space. The
boundary condition at the other end is less restrictive. It was argued in [29] that for
any choice of holomorphic Darboux coordinates {xi, yi} on the moduli space of flat ϵ-
connections, the second boundary condition is given by fixing all yi ≡0 (mod 2π). We
will come back to this in §3.2.3.
3.2
Moore-Seiberg monodromies on the gauged 3-sphere
We have learned in §3.1 that the space of Liouville conformal blocks forms a vector
bundle CB over the moduli space Mg,n of genus g Riemann surfaces Cg,n with n punctures.
In particular, when g = 0 we have that
M0,n = {(z1, . . . , zn) ∈(CP1)n : zi ̸= zj for i, j ∈{1, . . . , n}}/PSL(2, R),
(3.44)
where the group PSL(2, R) acts via M¨obius transformations. The vector bundle CB is
equipped with a projectively flat connection, and parallel transport around loops in Mg,n
thus induces nontrivial transformations in the space of conformal blocks. In fact, since
Mg,n = Tg,n/MCG(Cg,n),
(3.45)
where the Teichm¨uller space Tg,n is the universal cover of the moduli space Mg,n and
the mapping class group MCG(Cg,n) parametrises non-trivial loops in Mg,n, the space of
conformal blocks forms a projective representation of the mapping class group [39].
We have also explained in §3.1 that any pair of pants decomposition of the surface Cg,n
induces a basis for the space of Liouville blocks on Cg,n. For instance, on the 4-punctured
sphere, there are three standard choices known as the s-channel, the t-channel and the
u-channel basis corresponding to the three different pants decomposition. See fig. 13. The
generators of each basis are labeled by a single intermediate momentum α.
The mapping class group MCG(Cg,n) has a presentation with three generators known
as fusion, braiding, and modular (or S) moves. Given a pair of pants decomposition P
of the surface Cg,n, the action of each basic move on P produces a new pair of pants
decomposition P′. This structure may be captured in a groupoid whose vertices are all

Starting with a given pair of pants decomposition P, there may be several different
sequences of basic moves leading to another decomposition P′. Such consistency relations
between the three basic moves correspond to closed 2-cells in the Moore-Seiberg groupoid.
We refer the reader to [63] for more details. Since the S-move can be expressed in terms of
the other generators (and is not relevant anyhow when discussing genus 0 surfaces), we
will focus on the fusion and braiding moves from now on.

You may recognise the fusion matrices Fss′ in equation (3.55) as the connection matrices
for the hypergeometric differential equation. The braiding matrices Bss′ in eq. (3.59) agree
with this interpretation up to the factor of q1/2. Indeed, the Lax equation on the 3-punctured
sphere is described by a hypergeometric (or rather Riemann’s) differential equation. This
implies that the Moore-Seiberg parallel transport is identical to its semi-classical limit, up
to the overall factor of q.
3.2.2
BPZ equation and quantum oper connections
In the next few paragraphs, we interpret the BPZ equation as a quantum oper connection,
and argue that the fusion and braiding matrices (3.55) are computing the monodromies of
the quantum oper connection on the 3-punctured sphere. The paths corresponding to the
individual fusion and braiding matrices are illustrated in fig. 16.
We previously noted that the fusion matrices Fss′ in equation (3.55) are equal to the
connection matrices for the hypergeometric differential equation. However, observe that
the sequence of Moore-Seiberg moves (illustrated in fig. 17) transporting the degenerate
operator along a path trivial in homology (we have suppressed the internal momenta for

Oper connections
Before we work this out in detail, note that F1,2(z) transforms as a −1
2-differential
when b →0. As we explain in the following, this implies that the BPZ connection
reduces to an SL(2)-oper connection in the semi-classical limit. Indeed, it is well known
that the Lax equation transforms as an oper connection (see for instance [58]). Whereas
oper connections are defined in such a way that their defining equation stays invariant
under holomorphic coordinate transformations21, this is certainly not the case for the BPZ
connection at general values of b.
Indeed, let us find out how the following general, second-order differential equation

restoring the symmetry between the local exponents at the three punctures!
This confirms our claim that the asymmetry in the local exponents (3.62) is merely
an artifact of not carefully taking into account the transformation properties of the BPZ
equation. That is, the monodromies of the BPZ equation are honestly computed by the
braiding and fusion matrices.
Moreover, we also see that the BPZ equation should be considered a quantum oper
connection on the holomorphic bundle of conformal blocks F1,2(z), that only reduces to
a genuine oper connection in the semi-classical limit b →0. It then immediately follows
that any monodromy (or parallel transport, in general) computed through the braiding
and fusion matrices has an interpretation as a quantum monodromy (or quantum parallel
transport). We call this the Moore-Seiberg (MS) parallel transport.
Fenchel-Nielsen gauge
So far, we have learned that quantum parallel transports can be computed using
fusion and braiding matrices, that fusion matrices F correspond to connection coefficients
for the hypergeometric differential equation, and that braiding matrices B match with
local monodromy matrices up to a simple factor of q. In this subsection, we relate these
observations to parallel transports in terms of FN coordinates, as calculated in §2 using
W-nonabelianisation.

Comparing eq. (3.79) with eq. (2.32), we conclude that in the semiclassical limit, where
the quantum monodromy reduces to the Lax monodromy, the FN twist coordinates on the
gauged 3-sphere evaluate to a product of gamma-functions, as expressed in eq. (3.81).
We may thus phrase the relation between eq. (3.79) and eq. (2.32) as follows. The
monodromy representation of the semi-classical limit of the BPZ equation (i.e. Riemann’s
differential equation) on the 3-punctured sphere is equivalent to the monodromy represen-
tation in FN coordinates on the gauged 3-sphere, when the latter coordinates are evaluated
on the oper connection ∇Rie associated to Riemann’s differential equation. That is,

is not too important.)
3.2.3
Relation to the NRS proposal
The above expression (3.85) is closely related to the NRS proposal introduced in §3.1.3.
Recall that the NRS proposal states that the effective twisted superpotential eWeff of a class S
theory T[C] can be identified with the generating function of the Lagrangian submanifold
of (Lax) opers for a choice of holomorphic Darboux coordinates. For (non-exponentiated)
FN-type coordinates {ak, tk}, this proposal can be rephrased as
tk(∇Lax) = b ∂eWeff(⃗a)

Remark 3.4. We should be mindful about the following. The Lax connection ∇Lax on the
gauged 3-sphere reduces to Riemann’s connection ∇Rie on the 3-punctured sphere, but is
not strictly equal to it. In the limit where we ”ungauge” the punctures, the superpotential
on the gauged 3-sphere simplifies to the superpotential on the 3-punctured sphere, and
thus only retains its perturbative (i.e. classical + one-loop) part. If we therefore evaluate
the FN twist coordinates t on ∇Rie, instead of on the more general ∇Lax, we only expect to
reproduce the perturbative part of the complete eWeff on the gauged 3-sphere. We will get
back to the full statement in §3.3.2.

This correctly reproduces the one-loop contribution to eWeff associated with the gauged
3-sphere (up to quadratic terms in the masses mk). Indeed, the 4d N = 2 field theory
corresponding to the gauged 3-punctured sphere is the SU(2)3 gauge theory coupled to
a trifundamental half-hypermultiplet. The first line in the expression (3.93) enumerates
the one-loop contributions for the three gauge multiplets (with Coulomb parameters
m1,2,3). The second line computes the one-loop contribution for the trifundamental half-
hypermultiplet (with masses ±m1 ± m2 ± m3).25
Note that the expression (3.93) is also closely related to the square-root of the semi-

## Page 56


Relations similar to eq. (3.88) came up independently in the work of Lisovyy and
Naidiuk [65], where they were proven and named ”Trieste formulae”, as a reference to
analogous equations in [66]. Here, we want to emphasise that these relations are simply an
example of more general equations that can be extracted from the ”geometric recipe” [27],
based on the NRS proposal [31].
3.3
Moore-Seiberg monodromies on arbitrary surfaces
Thus far, we have only considered MS parallel transports within a gauged 3-punctured
sphere. In the following, we construct MS parallel transports on arbitrary surfaces through
the gluing construction.
Fix an arbitrary (possibly punctured) Riemann surface C together with a pants decom-
position P. Choose a path groupoid on C that respects the pants decomposition P and is
homotopic to the path groupoid illustrated in fig. 10 when restricted to any pair of pants.26
Then, the MS parallel transport along any path p on C may be computed by decomposing
the path p with respect to this groupoid, as
p = pゎpず· · · pり,
(3.97)
where each pろis an open path, embedded in a single pair of pants, that connects two
boundary components, and is thus homotopic to either pa,b,c or p′

As explained in §3.2, the MS parallel transport on 3-punctured spheres is identical to
its semi-classical limit, up to a factor of q. The concatenated MS parallel transport on C,
however, deviates from its semi-classical expression by the appearance of shifts in the
internal momenta (see for instance [40]). The underlying reason is that the momenta α
and α′ on either side of a degenerate operator V1,2 differ by a factor ±b/2. This means that
the internal momentum through a pants cycle shifts by this factor when the degenerate
operator is moved from one pair of pants to the other. This is illustrated in fig. 19 in the
example of a 4-punctured sphere. So, depending on whether the degenerate operator is to
the left or the right of the pants tube, the quantum monodromy around the pants cycle is
either

Aside: parallel transport versus monodromy invariants
Our emphasis in this paper is on the quantum parallel transport of vertex operators,
in contrast to its associated quantum monodromy invariants, which are also known as
Verlinde operators [41, 40]. In the semi-classical limit, Verlinde operators are simply given by
the conventional traces of the monodromies of the Lax connection. Quantum-mechanically,
though, they are computed by extending the quantum parallel transport computation
with two additional steps. First, we start with inserting the identity operator into a given
conformal block F, while resolving it into a pair of degenerate operators V1,2(z) using the
OPE
lim
z→0 V1,2(z)V1,2(0) ∼V1,3(0) + V1,1(0),
(3.117)
where V1,1(0) is the identity operator. Then, we take the degenerate operator V1,2(z)
around a given loop γ as before when computing the quantum parallel transport along
60

γ. And finally, we fuse the two degenerate vertex operators together, and project to the
channel of the identity operator. Altogether, this may be represented as a map
Lγ := π ◦PMS

(3.118)
where ι denotes the inclusion (and resolution) of the identity operator, and π denotes the
(fusion and) projection back to the identity operator (see fig. 23). The map Lγ is called a
Verlinde operator supported on γ, and it indeed computes a genuine quantum monodromy
invariant.
Remark 3.5. Previously, we had noted that the quantum parallel transport around a
trivial loop may not be trivial because of the projective nature of the BPZ connection.
Nevertheless, since Verlinde operators are quantum invariants, they are necessarily trivial
when supported on a trivial loop.
As an example, we compute the Verlinde operator supported on a trivial loop on a
three-punctured sphere corresponding to the computation (3.60). The sequence of moves
computing this operator is illustrated in fig. 23. After some straightforward algebra, we
verify that this indeed evaluates to 1.28

3.4
Spectral network as symmetry defect
In the final section of this chapter we comment on the physical role of spectral networks
in Liouville theory. We argue that the FN networks can be interpreted as topological
defects in the CFT.
3.4.1
Symmetry defects in QFT
A topological defect D is an (extended) operator in a quantum field theory whose
expectation value is invariant under small deformations of the support of the operator. In
the groundbreaking work [42], it was shown that generalized symmetries of a quantum
field theory correspond one-to-one with topological defects. We therefore also refer to a
topological defect as a symmetry defect.
A symmetry defect D of co-dimension d + 1 acts on an extended operator O of dimen-
sion d by wrapping the support of the operator around the defect. When the support of
the operator is shrunk to zero size, the relation between the defect and the operator can be
written in the form
D · O = R · O,
(3.134)
where R is the representation with which the generalised symmetry acts on O. Alterna-
tively, this can be expressed as
D′ · O = R · O · D′,
(3.135)
where the original defect D is split into two defects D′, each supported on half of the
original support of D, so that the operator O is sandwiched between the two operators D′
(see fig. 24). We should think of this as transporting the defect D across the operator O.
65


## Page 66


Conventional 0-form symmetries of an n-dimensional quantum field theory correspond
to topological defects with an (n −1)-dimensional support. The topological defect acts
on local operators according to the representation of the 0-form symmetry in which the
operator transforms.
Figure 24: On the left: the symmetry defect D (in dark blue) acts on the (extended) opera-
tor O (in red) by wrapping the support of the operator. On the right: the symmetry defect D′
acts on the (extended) operator O by moving through it.
3.4.2
Fenchel-Nielsen networks as symmetry defects
We now argue that Fenchel-Nielsen type networks act as 0-form symmetry defects in
Liouville theory, associated with its underlying SL(2) symmetry. Surely, spectral networks
are real 1-dimensional objects on a Riemann surface C. Furthermore, the abelianisation
map ψW, and more generally the q-nonabelianisation map ψq
W (as defined in [43]), is only
sensitive to the isotopy class of the spectral network W. In the following section §4, we also
show that the MS monodromies can be reproduced through the q-nonabelianisation proce-
dure with respect to an FN spectral network evaluated on conformal blocks (i.e. applied to
the BPZ connection).
Our aim is to then show that an FN network WFN acts on the degenerate vertex
operators Vp,q as
WFN · Vp,q = R · Vp,q · WFN,
(3.136)
where R is (a component of) the tensor product representation of the pth and the qth
symmetric tensor representations of SL(2) under which the degenerate vertex operators
Vp,q transform. Yet, note that acting with the spectral network WFN on Vp,q is equivalent to
moving the degenerate vertex operator Vp,q through WFN, and this multiplies the operator
with the corresponding quantum parallel transport.
As we have seen throughout this section (in the example of (p, q) = (1, 2)), this
parallel transport can be naturally brought in the FN gauge (3.79) with respect to a pants
decomposition of C. Hence, this allows us to conclude that FN spectral networks indeed
act as symmetry defects on the vertex operators V1,2, where the action is given by the
quantum parallel transport, in FN gauge, defined by the BPZ equation for V1,2. We expect
that this statement can be easily generalised to all degenerate vertex operators Vp,q.
4
Quantum parallel transport from q-nonabelianisation
As argued in the previous section, the Moore-Seiberg formalism realises a quantisa-
tion of the moduli space M(C, SL(2, C)) of flat connections in terms of difference op-
66


## Page 67


erators bTBPZ. Furthermore, these difference operators may be interpreted as quantum
exponentiated FN twist coordinates bT acting on conformal blocks.
More abstractly, the algebra of functions on the moduli space M(C, SL(2, C)) is quan-
tised by the skein algebra. To obtain a concrete realisation as an operator algebra acting on a
Hilbert space, one typically embeds the skein algebra
F : SkAlg(C, G) −→T
(4.1)
into a quantum torus algebra T. For non-abelian G, such an embedding is not canonical
and requires additional structure on the surface C, such as a triangulation [70, 71, 72], a
pants decomposition [48], or a spectral network [73, 43, 74]. The latter approach is known
as the q-nonabelianisation map [43], and defines the quantum analogue of the (ordinary)
nonabelianisation map.
In this section we apply the q-nonabelianisation map to FN-type networks. Our aim is
to find the precise relation between the Moore–Seiberg formalism and q-nonabelianisation.
Achieving this requires addressing two important issues. First, q-nonabelianisation in [43]
was primarily studied for spectral networks of FG-type, so that we need to extend the
formalism to spectral networks of FN-type. In particular, we need to determine how to
average over resolutions. Second, the main intention of [43] was to calculate quantum
invariants, whereas in this paper we are mostly interested in computing quantum parallel
transports.
In §4.1, we review the q-nonabelianisation map as defined in [43], while extending it to
open paths. In §4.2, we determine the average quantum S-matrices for the Fenchel-Nielsen
molecule from fig. 2. In §4.4, we extend the calculations to arbitrary surfaces C. We
conclude the section with an example.
4.1

an algebra homomorphism between the skein algebras

4.1.1
Rules
Whereas ordinary nonabelianisation only depends on the isotopy class of the spectral
network W, q-nonabelianisation makes use of the full WKB foliation - introduced in §2
as the set of all ij−trajectories (and not just the critical ones). The WKB foliation can be
conveniently captured in terms of a trivalent graph W L on C, known as the leaf space,
which intersects every ij−trajectory exactly once. As an example, fig. 25 illustrates the leaf
space of the FG network in fig. 1, which is isotopic to the FN network in fig. 2. Taking the
Cartesian product of the spectral network W (and its leaf space W L) with the interval I,
we get a 3d spectral network W3d ⊂C × I (and its leaf space W L
3d). We refer the reader to
[43, §5] for nice illustrations of 3d leaf spaces.

3d leaf space W L

The lifts ˜L are composed of three local pieces: direct lifts, detours and exchanges.
We are already familiar with direct lifts and detours. Exchanges are a novel feature of
q-nonabelianisation: when two segments of L intersect a single ij−trajectory at a fixed
height, i.e. when the projection of L onto W L
3d self-intersects, the lifts of the two segments
may exchange their strands along the ij−trajectory. This is illustrated in fig. 26. As we
shall see in the next subsection, exchanges will not play a role in this paper.
Figure 26: On the left: an exchange occurs when a link L (in red) self-intersects upon

3d (in yellow). In the middle: this is equivalent to two

case, depending on the orientations, the lifts ˜L (in light and dark green) of the two segments
may exchange their strands.
Both detour paths and exchanges are accompanied by an additional q-factor; direct
lifts carry no additional q-factors. All these q-factors contribute to the coefficients α(˜L).
Depending on the orientation of a detour path around the corresponding branch-point,
i.e. clockwise or anti-clockwise with respect to the orientation of ˜L in the height-direction, a
detour contributes a detour factor q±1/2. This is illustrated in fig. 27. Exchanges are instead
accompanied by a factor proportional to (q −q−1).
There is one other source of q-factors, the winding factors. To explain these, we consider
the restriction of the canonical vector field ∂t on C × I, for t ∈I, to the leaf space W L
3d. By
making small perturbations if necessary, a link L ⊂C × I can be positioned such that its
projection to W L
3d is tangential to the canonical vector field only at finitely many points.
We refer to these points as points of tangency. By making more small perturbations if
necessary, we can ensure that only the projection of L to W L
3d, and not L itself, is tangential
to the vector field ∂t at all points of tangency. This implies that the projection of L to C is
tangential to the WKB foliation at any point of tangency. This is illustrated in fig. 28.
The points of tangency contribute q-factors to α(˜L). In [43], these contributions were
separated into two local pieces: an overall winding factor and a sheet-dependent winding
factor:

to C × I may have more points of tangency than L. This happens precisely when ˜L
undergoes an exchange or takes a detour.
Note that any detour path winds around the branch-point and that its projection
to C × I necessarily has a point of tangency. This implies that there are two q-factors
associated with any detour: the detour-factor and the sheet-dependent winding-factor.
The clockwise (or anti-clockwise) detour introduces a factor of q−1/2 (or q1/2) into α(˜L).
Using the rules of fig. 30, the point of tangency contributes q1/2 (or q−1/2, respectively),
which therefore cancels the detour-factor exactly. This is illustrated in fig. 31. We may
therefore effectively ignore the q-factors associated to any detour path in our computations.
(This argument only depends on the local foliation structure around the branch-points,
and the conclusion is thus valid for any WKB foliation at a generic phase.)
This implies that, in the absence of exchanges, we may safely neglect all winding
contributions from points of tangency that are not common to both the base link L and its

This implies that q-nonabelianisation essentially quantises trace functions on the moduli
space of flat connections.
However, in this paper we are more interested in quantum parallel transport along
open paths rather than closed paths. Ordinary nonabelianisation allows us to write the
parallel transport of a flat SL(2, C)-connection along an open path p ⊂C as a matrix
valued in abelian parallel transports on the cover Σ. Analogously, we will define q-parallel
transport to be a matrix valued in abelian q-parallel transports on Σ. In a nutshell, q-parallel
transport is defined by applying the rules of q-nonabelianisation to “open paths” inside
C × I. In the remainder of this section, we make precise what we mean by this.
We define an open path inside C × I to be a link p ⊂C × I, with boundary ∂p ̸= ∅, that
is monotonously increasing (or decreasing) in the height-direction. In the skein literature,
such a configuration is called a tangle. We shall however simply refer to them as open
paths to emphasise that these are, in addition, monotonically increasing (or decreasing) in
73

the height-direction.
We denote the incoming and outgoing boundary components of p by ∂pin = ∂p

fixed finite set of points X ⊂C. For our purposes, we will take X to be the set of base points
of the path groupoid used in the nonabelianisation procedure. Note that since the links are
framed, each boundary point is also equipped with a framing vector. We can then consider
the Z[q±1]−module spanned by all upwards travelling open paths (i.e. monotonically
increasing along the height-direcion) stretching between C × {0} and C × {1} up to isotopy
and SL(2, C)−skein relations. We call this the space of open SL(2, C)−skeins

Remark 4.1. Formally, underlying this definition is a category called the skein category.
See [75, § 2] for a nice primer on skein theory. Below we collect a few facts that are relevant

Let Xin and Xout be two finite sets of (framed) points inside C. Then, the space spanned

Unlike the previously defined space of open skeins, the relative skein module additionally

Xin = Xout = ∅, the relative skein module recovers the usual SL(2, C)−skein algebra,
whereas the space of open skeins is the zero-dimensional vector space.
From these definitions, one can build a linear category whose simple objects are subsets

up to abelian gauge transformations at its end-points; only the abelian parallel transport
between marked points (where the vector bundle has been trivialised) is unambiguously
defined.33 This implies that the open path generators corresponding to open paths between
marked points play a special role. We will return to this in §4.3.

Simplifications
In this paper, we only consider q-parallel transports along connected
open paths. This means that we only consider open paths p ⊂C × I with a single
incoming boundary component zin and a single outgoing boundary component zout. So,
all the abelian q-parallel transports epij ⊂Σ × I stretch from zin,(i) on sheet i to zout,(j) on
sheet j, for i, j = 1, 2. In that case, we can write the q-parallel transport map as a matrix
Pq ∈Mat2×2(A) where the ijth entry of the q-parallel transport matrix comprises of all
the abelian q-parallel transports that start on sheet i and terminate on sheet j (similar
to ordinary nonabelianisation eq. (2.24)). Clearly, composition of q-parallel transports is
compatible with the multiplication structure in Mat2×2(A).
We can also slightly declutter our notations for lifts of connected open paths. When
unambiguous, we denote the open path generator corresponding to a lift epij by
Yij
p := Yepij
(4.21)
to signify that the generator corresponds to a lift of p going between sheet i and sheet j.
We also emphasise that, unless explicitly stated, Yij
p1Yjk

Yij

and not the generator corresponding to the disconnected open path p1 + p2.
Observe that in the q-parallel transport map, we are always able to apply the rules of
q-nonabelianisation to open paths that are monotonically increasing in the height-direction.
This necessarily means that there can be no self-intersections in the projection of the open
path to the leaf space, and consequently, q-parallel transport receives no contributions
from exchanges. It is this simplification that will allow us to define q-parallel transports
with respect to a Fenchel-Nielsen spectral network. Moreover, q-parallel transports and
ordinary nonabelianisation now share the same basic ingredients, vis. a vis. direct lifts and
detours. Notwithstanding this, the semi-classical limit of the q-parallel transport matrix
(i.e. setting q = 1 and replacing all the open path generators with their semi-classical limits)
recovers the usual parallel transport eq. (2.24). It is in this sense that q-parallel transport
“quantises” the space of SL(2, C)−parallel transports on C, or equivalently, the space of
SL(2, C)−monodromy representations on C.
Finally, we remark that it becomes crucial to handle the framing of the link with more
care in the presence of boundaries. For a closed link, the framing factor is an invariant. By
that, we mean that without “cutting” the link, there is no way to change the framing of
76


## Page 77


the link. However, this is no longer true for open paths. We can change the framing of an
open path by changing the boundary framing. Thus, it becomes important to define the
boundary framing of the open paths carefully, and ensure compatibility of framings while
gluing. This is implicitly assumed in the rest of this section.
4.1.3
3d Chern-Simons theory
In this paragraph, we briefly remind the reader about the physical relevance of q-
nonabelianisation and q-parallel transport. Recall that the AGT correspondence states that
the supersymmetric partition function of a 4d N = 2 class S SU(2) theory, constructed by
compactifying the 6d (2, 0)-theory of type A1 on a Riemann surface C, in the squashed
4-sphere background can be identified with the Liouville correlation function on C. We
obtain a slightly different perspective by considering the squashed 4-sphere as a non-trivial
fibration of the squashed 3-sphere over an interval I¯h,
S3
b ,→S4
b →[−¯h, ¯h] =: I¯h,
that degenerates at the boundary of the interval. Then, similar to the AGT correspondence
(i.e. the 4d-2d correspondence), there exists a 3d-3d correspondence [76, 77] between a
3d N = 2 SU(2) gauge theory on the squashed 3-sphere S3
b and the SL(2, C)-Chern-
Simons theory on C × I¯h.34 The interval I¯h (after rescaling) defines the height direction in
q-nonabelianisation.
The degenerating fibres at the boundary points define non-trivial boundary conditions
for the Chern-Simons theory at the ends of the interval. These boundary conditions
only depend on the Riemann surface C. Suppose we denote the incoming and outgoing
boundaries by Cin = C × {−¯h} and Cout = C × {¯h}, respectively. Then, the space of
boundary conditions on Cin is the space of chiral Liouville blocks on C, and the space
of boundary conditions on Cout is the space of anti-chiral Liouville blocks on C. The
Chern-Simons theory partition function defines a pairing between the two spaces:
ZCS : CB(Cin) × CB(Cout) →C.
Leaving aside potential complications associated with the dualisability of these spaces, we
can write this as an endomorphism on the space of chiral Liouville blocks:
ZCS : CB(Cin) →CB(Cin).
Since the 4d N = 2 theory on the squashed 4-sphere is trivial away from the poles, it
follows that the Chern-Simons theory is trivial along the interval I¯h [78]. We thus conclude
that the pairing defined by the Chern-Simons partition function simply reproduces the
Liouville correlation function. This is the standard AGT correspondence, and can be
directly verified in the 4d theory through supersymmetric localisation [78].
Alternatively, we could engineer a scenario where the 3d theory is not quite trivial
along the interval direction. For instance, suppose we introduce a compact Wilson loop Lγ
34Recall that complex Chern-Simons theory depends on two parameters: the quantised level k ∈Z and a
continuous parameter s ∈R or iR. The above duality holds for k = 1 and s = 1−b2
1+b2 .
77

supported on a loop γ inside C × I¯h. If the loop γ is isotopic to a loop inside a fixed height
slice C × {t}35, the Chern-Simons pairing
ZCS[Lγ] : CB(Cin) →CB(Cin)
represents the action of a Verlinde loop operator, supported on γ, on the space of Liouville
blocks on C.
Dually, in the 3d supersymmetric gauge theory, the Wilson loop Lγ describes a non-
