Dualising again, the above UV-IR map describes a sum of compact Wilson loop eL in a
GL(1, C)−Chern-Simons theory on Σ × I¯h. From a 2d perspective, the abelian Wilson loop
is an abelian Verlinde operator. Basically, this is the underlying physical interpretation
behind the q-nonabelianisation map. A more detailed explanation of the 3d UV-IR map,
and various other related facts, can be found in [43, Section 1].
There is yet another way to define a non-trivial endomorphism on the space of con-
formal blocks. In the 3d theory, the boundary CFT operators are lifted to non-compact
Wilson lines Lopen starting on Cin and terminating on Cout. For a trivial bulk theory, the
non-compact Wilson lines are stationary along the C direction, so that they do not interact
with each other in an interesting way. However, we can modify the bulk theory by allowing
for the braiding and fusion of the non-compact Wilson lines. This is simply described by
the Moore-Seiberg transformations on the space of conformal blocks. In other words, the
Chern-Simons pairing
ZCS[Lopen] : CB(Cin) →CB(Cin)
describes the action of the mapping class group on the space of Liouville blocks on C.
We know that these transformations are incredibly complicated for generic vertex
operators in the CFT. However, we also know that the situation is much more manageable
for degenerate operators. Let us therefore fix all the non-compact Wilson lines for generic
representations to be spectators (these correspond to the insertions at the punctures),
and only allow the braiding and fusion of non-compact Wilson lines for degenerate
operators. Identifying the latter with the open skeins on C, the UV-IR map applied to
such a configuration of non-compact Wilson lines inside the Chern-Simons theory gives
precisely the q-parallel transport map.36 Note that the marked points in X are all the
possible (inequivalent) insertion points of degenerate operators in C in the presence of the
other generic operators.
35Line defects Lγ that are not isotopic to loops in a fixed height slice are called fat line defects in [43].
36More precisely, we identify a non-compact Wilson line corresponding to a (1, 2) degenerate operator
with an open path in C × I labelled by the defining representation of SL(2, C) (also see eq. (3.36)).

Abstractly, q-parallel transport is a formal sum of open path generators Yep. However,
the above picture allows us to evaluate the q-parallel transport map on the Hilbert space
of the abelian Chern-Simons theory – the space of abelian (Heisenberg) conformal blocks
on Σ. That is, we can realise the open path generators Yep as operators on abelian conformal

In principle, knowledge of the quantum S-matrix is sufficient to compute q-parallel
transports along any path inside the 3-punctured sphere. Suppose we would like to
compute the q-parallel transport along an open path p ⊂C × I which crosses a double wall
exactly once. Then, we may decompose p = pι ◦pf where pι is the segment that lies before
the crossing and pf is the segment after the crossing. The q-parallel transport along the
segments pι and pf only consists of direct lifts. Then, by the locality of q-parallel transport,
we have
Pq

(4.31)
where we compose the individual q-parallel transports along pι and pf with the appropriate
quantum FN S-matrix. For an open path p that crosses a double trajectory more than
once, we apply the same prescription at every crossing. We discuss the extension of this
construction to arbitrary surfaces in §4.4.
Before we derive the expression eq. (4.26), it is instructive to verify its consistency.
One of the most important tests of consistency of q-nonabelianisation is invariance under
two kinds of isotopies: isotopy across a critical trajectory and isotopy across a branch-
point. For a single critical ij−trajectory, this was already established in [43]. We shall now
demonstrate the same for the double wall.
Remark 4.2. We believe that eq. (4.26) is the “simplest” quantisation of the semiclassical
S-matrix that is compatible with q-nonabelianisation. Indeed, observe that the coefficients
α, α′, β, β′ and γ that appear in the S-matrices are only constituted from the exponentiated
masses (i.e. no qs), and thus, can be computed directly in the semiclassical limit. Moreover,
if we were to start with an ansatz where each coefficient in the quantum S-matrix is
identical to its semi-classical counterpart up to an overall factor qaij, then only imposing
isotopy invariance across double walls and branch-points is sufficient to fix the q-factors
as in eq. (4.26).

12−critical trajectory emanating from the top branch-point (corresponding to the unprimed

q-factors. This agrees with the form of eq. (4.25).

the q-nonabelianisation of the two paths p1 and p2, we simply count the q-factors of each
lift. The (projections of the) various lifts of p1 are illustrated in fig. 39. The detours of the
path p1 are captured by SqFN, and it is also easy to see that p1 picks up no winding factors.
So, only the lift starting and ending on sheet 1 is accompanied by a non-trivial q-factor,
namely, q−1 (c.f. eq. (4.26)), and the rest of the lifts receive no q-contributions.
We now compare this with the open path lifts of p2. The detours of p2 are encoded in
(SqFN)−1. So, in total, there are three potential sources of q-factors for its lifts:
1. a winding factor q−1 if the winding takes place on sheet 2,
2. a detour-factor q for every detour from sheet 2 to sheet 1 (c.f. eq. (4.26)), and finally,
3. a skein-factor q−1 from resolving the self-intersection of a lift with two detours.
The lifts of p2 that start and end on sheet 1 either undergo no detours or two detours. They
are illustrated in fig. 40. The lift that undergoes no detour is accompanied by a winding
factor q−1 that matches that of p1. For the lift that undergoes two detours, both detours are
from sheet 1 to 2, and do not contribute any q-factors. Furthermore, the winding factor is
also 1. But it does self-intersect, resolving which we get a factor of q−1.
85


## Page 86


Figure 38: Isotopy move across a branch-point.
On the other hand, the lifts of p2 that start and end on sheet 2 also undergo no detours
or two detours. However, its q-factors are all flipped. The lift that undergoes no detours
now winds on sheet 1, and hence, carries a trivial q-factor. Likewise, the lift that undergoes
two detours, now picks up a winding factor q−1, a detour-factor q2 and a skein factor q−1
from resolving the self-intersection, which all effectively cancel each other.
Figure 39: Projections of the lifts of the open path p1 to C with source and target sheets
indicated. Only the lift from sheet 1 to sheet 1 has a non-trivial q factor.
Figure 40: Projections of the lifts of the open path p2 to C with source and target sheets
indicated.
The lifts of p2 that start on sheet 1 and end on sheet 2 either undergo a 1 →2 detour
at the “first wall” or a 2 →1 detour at the “second wall” (see fig. 40). First of all, there
is no q-contribution from self-intersections in this case. Second, the former lift has no
detour-factor and winding factor while the latter is accompanied by a winding factor q−1
86


## Page 87


and a detour-factor q which cancel each other. Ultimately, both lifts are accompanied by
trivial q-factors. The same holds for lifts of p2 that start on sheet 2 and end on sheet 1.
From this, we can conclude straight away that q-parallel transport along p1 and p2 are the
same. This establishes consistency of the average quantum FN S-matrices under isotopies
across branch-points.
Remark 4.4. The argument above also extends to a small closed loop L around the simple
branch-point. Since the local foliation of C around such a branch-point is topologically
the same for any phase, it is always possible to isotope the loop such that there are no
exchanges. By a further isotopy, the loop can also be arranged so that it is travelling
upwards when crossing any trajectory (see fig. 41 for an example). Let us then decompose
L = Lu ◦Ld into a component Lu (shown in dark green in fig. 41) that is an upwards-
travelling open path and Ld = L\Lu (shown in light green). The q-nonabelianisation of L
is then given by
ψq(L) = ψq(Lu) ◦ψq(Ld) = Pq(Lu) ◦ψq(Ld).
(4.40)
Through construction, ψq(Ld) only consists of direct lifts, and we have already established
that the q-parallel transport map is invariant under an isotopy across a branch-point. It
then follows that ψq(L) is also invariant under an isotopy across a simple branch-point.
Figure 41: On the left: example of a small loop (in green) around a simple branch-point,
projected onto C. On the right: the same configuration (without the spectral network
trajectories and the branch-cuts), but now projected onto a plane in C × I, perpendicular to
one of the spectral network trajectories.

4.3
Quantum Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates
The semi-classical limits of the abelian q-parallel transports Yij
L/R are not gauge-invariant,
and therefore do not define functions on the moduli space of flat connections and cannot
be consistently quantised. In contrast, the abelian parallel transports M1,2,3 along the lifts
of the based pants cycles a1,2,3 ⊂C, and the abelian parallel transports Ta,b,c along the lifts
of the paths pa,b,c ⊂C (defined in §2.3) are gauge-invariant (since we fixed a trivialisation
at the marked points), and thus can be quantised. Consequently, the semi-classical limits
of the generators
Yii
a1,a2,a3
and
Yij
pa,pb,pc,
(4.63)
are gauge-invariant. Moreover, since any gauge-invariant abelian parallel transport can be
expressed in terms of the spectral coordinates, it follows that the open path generators (4.63)
form a basis for the space of all abelian q-parallel transports whose semi-classical limit is
gauge-invariant. Thus, the (linear combination of) generators eq. (4.63) (corresponding to
the symplectic basis of 1-cycles (3.87)) have the right characteristics to be interpreted as

Remark 4.6. There is a subtlety in writing down the q-parallel transport matrices Pq in
eq. (4.64). We have assumed that the open path approaches its end-points transversely to
the WKB foliation (see fig. 46). However, one could equally well choose a path that ends
tangentially to the WKB foliation. In the latter case, the sheet-dependent winding factor is
different. Consequently, special care is required when composing Pq-matrices to construct
general q-parallel transport matrices.

are identical in the semi-classical limit, their quantisations are elements of different quantum algebras.

Remark 4.7. As we will see in §5.1, the space of genus zero Heisenberg conformal blocks
(for any number of generic vertex operator insertions with fixed momenta) is 1-dimensional.
Then, any operator that commutes with the action of the Heisenberg-Verlinde operators
(i.e. doesn’t modify the vertex operator insertions) must act multiplicatively.
This analysis extends even in the presence of multiple degenerate operators (inserted
at any of the points in X). The crucial thing to note is that all basis generators eq. (4.63)
always commute with the action of the Heisenberg-Verlinde operators. Then, for any
number of composable open path generators, not necessarily associated to connected open
paths, we can immediately conclude that the action of these generators is multiplicative.
In particular, this implies that the action of the quantum FN coordinates on the gauged
3-sphere is multiplicative, and that the multiplicative constant is the corresponding abelian
parallel transport evaluated on the BPZ connection. It is in this sense that we refer to the
open path generators

We can similarly obtain the CFT-abelianisation of any other choice of basis for CB(C) by
displacing the position of the degenerate operator insertion to any of the other punctures.
Furthermore, more general open path generators Yep (with distinct end-points) appear
in the description of basis change transformations on the space of Liouville blocks as in
eq. (4.64).
Remark 4.8. Note that CFT-abelianisation treats generic and degenerate vertex operator
insertions differently. This differential treatment can be explained through the 6d (2, 0)-
theory in the background R4 × C that sources the AGT correspondence.
In the 6d theory, the punctures on C are the insertion points for codimension-2 defects
(space-filling defects inside R4) that describe the matter content of the 4d N = 2 theory on
R4 [61]. Under the action of the UV-IR map described in §4.1.3, the low-energy limit of the
codimension-2 defects fixes the flavour charges of the 4d theory on the Coulomb branch.
Importantly, this is a one-to-one mapping between the UV and IR limits of the 4d theories.
The degenerate vertex operator insertions, in contrast, correspond to codimension-4
defects in the 6d (2, 0)-theory that specify a canonical surface defect in the 4d N = 2 theory
[41]. At low energies, this surface defect admits a discrete set of vacua (IR surface defects)
which the UV-IR map selects from. That is, the UV-IR map is not one-to-one when acting on
surface defects. In particular, the two basis elements in eq. (4.79) can be explicitly identified
with the two brane configurations engineering IR surface defects in the low-energy limit
of the 4d N = 2 theory (see for instance [81, Fig. 14]).
For a general surface C

4.3.2
Relation between X and Y-coordinates
Although the quantum torus generators Xγ and the open path generators Yep are valued
in different algebras, it is possible to obtain the X-coordinates from the Y-coordinates.
Semi-classically, the trace function is simply the trace of the parallel transport computed
for a choice of base-point, and for an abelian connection there is no distinction between the
parallel transport around a (based) loop and its trace. Likewise in the quantum theory, the
open path generator Yep for any open path ep, such that epΣ is a loop based at zb ∈Σ, can be
decomposed in terms of Xγ, where γ represents the homology class of epΣ, and the identity
open path Yii
zb. This is precisely how we were able to read off the quantum FN coordinates
from q-parallel transports.
Alternatively, the trace can be computed as follows. Trivialize the abelian gauge
bundle at two nearby points zb, z′
b ∈Σ and use this trivialization to identify the fiber
at z′
b with the dual of the fiber at zb via the standard evaluation pairing. The semi-
classical parallel transport along the closed path pΣ then defines an endomorphism of
98


## Page 99


the fiber, and contracting its output with its input using the pairing yields the ordinary
trace of the holonomy. This is the semi-classical translation of the computation of a
Heisenberg–Verlinde operator in the abelian CFT.

4.4
On q-parallel transport for arbitrary Riemann surfaces
Consider an arbitrary Riemann surface C equipped with a pants decomposition P. Fix
a path groupoid that respects P and is homotopic to the path groupoid on the (gauged)
three-punctured sphere when restricted to each pair of pants. Let p be an arbitrary open

may be computed by decomposing the path p as

(4.90)
where each pろis an (connected) open path embedded in a single pair of pants. Since the
knowledge of the quantum FN S-matrix is sufficient to compute the q-parallel transport
along any path inside the three-punctured sphere, the only remaining ingredient in the
computation of the q-parallel transport map on an arbitrary Riemann surface is the gluing
operation.
Recall the definition of a gauged puncture: we cut open a small disk around a puncture,
and fix a marked point on the boundary. It is convenient to think of the thickened boundary
component as an annulus, and of the marked point as lying on the inner boundary of the
annulus (see fig. 51). Lifting this configuration to the cover Σ, this allows us to define an
ordering between the closed pants-cycles and the open 1-cycles with respect to the marked
point. The 1-cycles are defined such that they can be collapsed onto the boundary without
any obstruction from the open paths. This is illustrated in fig. 51.
To glue two gauged punctures, we identify their boundary components as well as their
marked points. However, this introduces the lateral ordering ambiguity illustrated in
fig. 52. Recall from fig. 43 that γ was the loop encircling the puncture while γ′ was the
loop on the other side of the open path. On a tube though, there is no canonical way to
distinguish between γ and γ′. The best we can do is to fix a choice of γ versus γ′ on each
pants-tube, and stay consistent with that choice. This is the (open) skein analogue of the
CFT ambiguity in the choice of internal momenta on the pants-tube with a degenerate
operator, as illustrated in fig. 19.
101

Remark 4.10. It is not surprising that the winding factors of q-parallel transport and
MS parallel transport do not coincide. Whereas q-nonabelianisation picks up a (sheet-
dependent) winding factor from every turn with respect to the foliation, the Moore-Seiberg
parallel transport only picks up a (uniform overall) winding factor around punctures.
However, we do ultimately expect the two results to completely agree for closed loops
(corresponding to Verlinde loop operators).
Nonetheless, it is fairly straight-forward to translate between the two quantum parallel
transports. For instance, given a (fully evaluated) q-parallel transport along any non-self-
intersecting curve inside the 3-punctured sphere, we can recover the corresponding MS
parallel transport by “forgetting” the additional winding factors and including an overall
factor of q±1 for every turn around a puncture. In the other direction, we forget the overall
q-factor in the MS parallel transport, and include sheet-dependent winding factors (as in
fig. 32) from all windings with respect to the foliation.
Yet, ignoring winding factors, we expect q-parallel transports and MS parallel transports
to be in full agreement. Especially, the composite expressions for Pq

5
Liouville blocks from spectral networks
The ultimate ambition in this section is to construct Liouville conformal blocks from
just the data of a spectral network. The initial perspective that we take to achieve this is
that of the free-field representation, in which Liouville theory is described in terms of a
free boson field supplemented by non-local interaction terms, called screening charges,
which are specified by a set of contours on the surface C.
In this section, we relate the choice of contour system to the choice of a spectral network.
In particular, we show that contour systems for standard Liouville conformal blocks are
associated with networks of Fenchel-Nielsen type, while we propose that contour systems
for Fock-Goncharov type networks are associated with more general ”Goncharov-Shen”
conformal blocks.
We start in §5.1 with introducing the free-field formalism, while emphasising the role
of the screening charges. In §5.2 we phrase free-field conformal blocks, with one type of
screening charges, as Penner-type matrix model integrals. We interpret the matrix model as
a generalised Landau-Ginzburg model and identify the spectral cover Σ using the matrix
model potential Wmat. The spectral cover is singular at finite rank N of the matrix model,
and smoothens out in the large N limit.
Penner-type matrix models have long been known to be related to Liouville conformal
blocks. In particular, interest in the free-field description of Liouville theory [33, 32] and its
relation to β-deformed matrix models and the AGT conjecture [10], was revived with the
appearance of the Dijkgraaf-Vafa paper [34]. Many aspects of the correspondence were
fleshed out in subsequent years, culminating in proofs of certain cases [82, 83, 14]. Even
so, with the exception of the follow-up paper [84] with Cheng, the role of the contour
system seems largely unexplored. In §5.4.3 we argue that the correspondence between
Penner-type matrix models and Liouville conformal blocks only works if the contours are
chosen with respect to a degenerate network of Fenchel-Nielsen type.
However, in the course of this analysis we stumble upon some (known) complications
with the Penner-type matrix models. In particular, resurgence properties of the large N
matrix model partition function cannot be reproduced from the single matrix model. As
a remedy, Marino, Schiappa and Schwick propose in [85] to consider 2-matrix models,
which from the free-field perspective correspond to the inclusion of both types of Liouville
screening charges. While this seems promising, we argue that the difficulties arise because
the spectral curve of the Penner-type matrix model is singular. Indeed, the contours of
these matrix models are defined in terms of trajectories of a degenerate spectral network,
whose topology changes are restricted.
106


## Page 107


Instead, we make the case that the free-field description should be altered to find a
complete description of conformal blocks, in a way that is compatible with the c = 1
construction of [35]. That is, we propose to consider the free-field formalism with respect
to the smooth spectral curve and to insert the screening charges with respect to a non-
degenerate spectral network. In §5.4 we describe the resulting CFT abelianisation map,
which compared to [35] is enriched with the appearance of the Maulik-Okounkov R-
matrix [44].

extend the discussion later in this section to general Riemann surfaces through the gluing
procedure.

Gluing free-field correlation functions
Conformal blocks on Riemann surfaces of higher genus can be obtained using the gluing
construction introduced in §3.1 provided there are no contours terminating on the puncture
that is being closed. (In particular, if free-field conformal blocks were defined through
(degenerate) FN contours, they can always be glued together.) The one ingredient that
changes from the previous discussion is the momentum conservation constraint (5.23).
The Liouville momenta of vertex operator insertions on a genus g surface must now obey

taking a genus zero surface with 2g + n vertex operator insertions, and sewing g pairs of
insertions using the plumbing construction from §3.1. Note that this requires each pair
of vertex operators that is being sewn together to carry conjugate momenta α and Q −α.
In other words, each pair that is being sewn together contributes a total charge Q to the
momentum conservation relation. The genus zero momentum conservation constraint
then reads

5.2
Penner-type matrix models and spectral networks
As we mentioned above, a conventional choice of contours in the free-field literature
is a combination of Pochhammer contours. Instead, in this section we argue that it is
more natural to consider contour systems defined by (degenerate) spectral networks. We
motivate this through the correspondence between free-field correlators and partition
functions of β-deformed matrix models (see for instance the discussion in [34, 89] with
references to [49, 50]).

114


## Page 115


Remark 5.4. The resulting matrix model partition function may be thought of as a multi-
dimensional version of the partition function of a 2d Landau-Ginzburg (LG) model. In
the latter models, the critical points can be identified with the (finitely many) vacua of the
2d N = (2, 2) QFT. For any fixed phase ϑ, the associated collection of Lefschetz thimbles
defines a basis of 2d LG partition functions and jumps in the Lefschetz thimbles can be
interpreted as 2d BPS solitons interpolating between the vacua. As explained in detail in
[80, §5.6], mathematically this wall-crossing can be described elegantly through the exact
WKB analysis.
In the current context, the critical points and the Lefschetz thimbles similarly have
an interpretation in terms of the underlying 4d N = 2 QFT. As will become clear in the
next subsection, the distribution of eigenvalues amongst the critical points parametrises
Coulomb vacua, labeled by ⃗a, of the 4d N = 2 QFT. The collection of matrix model
partition functions

Zβ−mat

for fixed phase ϑ, forms an infinite-dimensional basis of Liouville blocks (the labels⃗a in
this context correspond to Liouville momenta⃗α). Jumps in the integration cycle Γϑ induce
jumps in the partition function, which in this setup are associated with 4d BPS particles
present in the spectrum of the 4d N = 2 QFT at the chosen vacuum⃗a.

Remark 5.6. Unitarity in Liouville theory requires b > 0 (see the discussion around

β > 0 over β < 0. For β < 0 there are complications in the definition of the Lefschetz
thimbles, and also a naive analysis of eq. (5.49) suggests that both types of screening
charges would need to be inserted along the same downward trajectories. Unfortunately,
we are unable to resolve this (apparent) contradiction at the moment. Instead, in the
following we will treat Liouville theory as a β-deformation away from β = 1 (as seems to
be the standard approach in the physics literature), while (implicitly) assuming that β > 0
and then analytically continuing in β.
Convergence of the free-field integrals
For generic θ, the integration cycles Γθ end on punctures of C. Then, the contour
integral (5.40) is well-behaved only if the momenta αk are appropriately constrained. From
the perspective of the free-field formalism, we require that the OPE between the screening
operator S± and the vertex operator insertion at the puncture is regular [32, 33, 35]. For
instance, suppose that a screening contour γ supporting S+ ends on a primary vertex
operator Vα. Then, the limit
lim
y→z S+(y)Vα(z) = lim
y→z(y −z)−2αb : S+(y)Vα(z) :
(5.51)

(5.52)

In particular, if an FN-type trajectory winds in the counter-clockwise direction around
a puncture, this implies that
ℑ(αb) < 0.
(5.53)
That is, Q+ (with respect to such a trajectory) is only allowed to end on vertex operators Vα
with ℑ(αb) < 0. If instead ℑ(αb) > 1 + ℜ(b2), we may consider replacing Vα(z) with
VQ−α(z), since the OPE
S+(y)VQ−α(z) = (y −z)−2(Q−α)b : S+(y)VQ−α(z) :
(5.54)
is convergent in this case. Likewise, Q−(y) (with respect to the above FN-type trajectory)
can only end on Vα(z) if ℑ(α/b) < 0, and on VQ−α(z) if ℑ(α/b) > 1 + ℑ(b−2). We collect
these constraints in table 1 below.
Remark 5.7. Even though the integral is convergent subject to the constraints in table 1, one
may wonder whether the resulting free-field correlators preserve conformal invariance. To
check this, we need to make sure that the conformal Ward identities for a given free-field
correlator are invariant under adding screening charges.
Recall that these identities are derived by inserting η(z)T(z) into the correlator (see the
discussion around eq. (3.23)). In particular, the resulting correlator should be a meromor-
phic function in z, say f (z), that is holomorphic away from the vertex operator insertions.

119


## Page 120


Remark 5.8. The relation between free-field correlators and β-deformed matrix models
can be extended to any Riemann surface C by allowing dWmat to be a generic closed
1-form λ on C with first-order poles at the punctures [34].52 Such a generic differential
has 2g −2 + n first-order zeroes, which corresponds to the number of pair of pants in any
pants decomposition of the surface C. The eigenvalue distribution amongst those zeroes
thus generates 2g −2 + n filling fractions (also called DV phases in [96]). Together with the
g additional moduli of the differential λ and the overall Liouville momentum constraint,
the 2g −2 + n filling fractions combine into the correct number of 3g −3 + n independent
Coulomb parameters⃗a for the 4d N = 2 theory T[C].
5.3
Examples of free-field correlators
In this section we work through a few examples of free-field correlators. We start
with the Gaussian matrix model, which is defined with respect to a particularly simple
degenerate network: the spectral covering has one double branch-point and changing
the phase ϑ merely rotates the network. We comment on how the large N limit might
be thought of as introducing a non-trivial dependence on ϑ, and identify the large N
partition function at the Fenchel-Nielsen phase with the 4d N = 2 partition function of a
free hypermultiplet.
We continue with studying three and four-point correlators on the sphere. The asso-
ciated families of degenerate networks for these theories do undergo topology changes
when the phase ϑ is varied, but fewer than the resolved family. Using the additional
insights gained in the previous example, we argue that the large N matrix model partition
functions at the FN phase precisely reproduce the corresponding Liouville blocks (that
is, square-root of 3-point function times Virasoro conformal block). Along the way we
also analyse examples of free-field correlators that are defined with respect to FG-type
networks.

the anti-topological string normalisation, whereas eq. (5.94) computes the same partition
function in the CFT normalisation.

Remark 5.11. Following the line of this paper, it would have been natural to instead
consider the matrix model partition function (5.49), which is defined with respect to both
types of Liouville screening charges, and therefore contains integrals along both types
of spectral network trajectories. Even so, the underlying spectral network would be
degenerate and does not undergo any topology changes when varying the phase. We
therefore do not expect this generalised partition function to encode Stokes jumps (at finite
N) either (although it may resolve issues at large N [85]). We will argue in §5.4 that it
is more promising to define a new matrix model partition function with respect to the
smooth spectral curve Σ.

and flow into the punctures at y = 0 and y = 1, together with two vertical 21-trajectories
that start at the branch-point y∗and flow into the puncture at y = ∞. If the phase is varied,
the trajectories start winding around the punctures. The phase ϑ = π/2 (mod π) is special
since all trajectories of the network are compact. That is, at this phase the degenerate
network is of FN type.
All eigenvalues of the matrix model are associated with the single branch-point, and
run along the union of the two 12-trajectories of the network Wϑ(ϕ2). The matrix model
partition function is thus given by

drawn into the punctures, the matrix model partition function stays invariant as well.
Fenchel-Nielsen phase
If we tune the phase towards ϑ = π/2 (mod π), the number of

change in the family of spectral networks precisely at the phase ϑ = π/2 (mod π). At this
phase all trajectories are compact, and the network Wϑ(ϕ2) is of degenerate FN type. If we
cross the critical phase ϑ = π/2 (mod 2π), the matrix model partition function jumps from

Remark 5.13. Instead of a Fenchel-Nielsen contour, we could also have considered a
Pochhammer contour around the punctures at y = 0 and y = 1. Such a contour equally
has the property that it stays away from the punctures, but the corresponding free-field
integral results in yet a different normalisation of the 1-loop factor (see for instance eq. (B.8)
for the evaluation of this contour when N = 1).
Remark 5.14. Note that the matrix model partition function in all three normalisations
obeys difference equations similar to the quantum NRS relation (3.133) on the 3-punctured
sphere.
Resolved spectral networks
As we reviewed in §5.2.2, the singular spectral curve Σsing
desingularises into a smooth spectral curve Σ in the limit N →∞, in which we perform our
analysis with respect to the effective action Seff that also takes into account Vandermonde
interactions (see eq. (5.68)). In particular, Σ has non-zero periods
I

In the above computations, we have computed the matrix model partition functions for
contour systems Γϑ defined with respect to degenerate spectral networks Wdeg on Σsing.
Yet, we have anticipated that these spectral networks resolve in the large N limit, and hence
undergo additional flip transformations, when formulating the partition function ˜Z in
(5.108). The final result (5.114) is in perfect agreement with the Liouville 3-point function.
Here we ask ourselves what the resolved spectral networks look like, in particular at a
FN phase ϑFN. Clearly, the resolved networks must be defined with respect to the resolved
differential λ on the smoothened Σ. Suppose that we had started off with a degenerate
network Wdeg of FN type. Then Wdeg would respect the given pants decomposition of C,
and hence obey the relation

Figure 59: Two flip transitions connecting FG networks defined by ϕ2 in eq. (5.127) for
µ0 = µq = µ1 = 1 and q = 1/5 at phase ϑ = −0.75π, ϑ = −0.68π and ϑ = −0.57π,

FN-type network at phase ϑ = 0.
Flips
The degenerate network Wϑ
deg undergoes an infinite series of flips when varying
the phase ϑ from −π to −π/2. The first two flip transitions are illustrated in Fig. 59. The
first flip interchanges the 21-trajectory that originates from the double branch-point on the
right and runs to the puncture at y = q with the 12-trajectory running below it. In this
process the B-cycle on the cover Σsing, which connects the two double branch-points, stays
invariant. The second flip interchanges the new 21-trajectory with the other 12-trajectory
that originates from the double branch-point on the left. This leaves the 1-cycle invariant
that runs in between the latter trajectories and connects the double branch-points. This
sequence generalises straight-forwardly. It would be exciting to be able to compute explicit
expressions for these flip transitions, with the aim of finding expressions for 4-point
correlators at FG phases.
136


## Page 137


5.3.4
General correlators
The above relation between free-field correlators at the FN phase and Liouville blocks
can be easily generalised to arbitrary rational curves C. Indeed, fix a directed pair of pants
decomposition, in which two boundary components of every pair of pants are labeled as
ingoing and one boundary component as outgoing, such that (internal) ingoing boundary
components are glued to outgoing ones. Then there is a unique degenerate FN network
WFN
deg that respects this pants decomposition, up to isotopy and the Z2-choice of labelings.
This network is isotopic to the degenerate FN molecule from fig. 5 on each pair of pants,
and oriented such that the two punctures at finite distance correspond to the ingoing
boundary components and the puncture at infinity to the outgoing one.
Now, write down the most general meromorphic 1-form λsing on C that has a first-order
pole with residue
µk = −2b¯hαk
(5.151)
at each of the ingoing punctures zk, and reproduces the isotopy class of the degenerate
network WFN
deg for µk > 0 at a certain phase ϑFN (mod π). Define the matrix model potential
Wmat through the relation
dWmat = λsing,
(5.152)
and specify the matrix model contour ΓFN as the union of all 12-trajectories of the degener-
ate network defined by λsing.
Since all computations will be similar to the ones performed for the 4-punctured sphere,
we propose that the large N matrix model partition function

(Note that this proposal does build on the slightly ad-hoc argument above eq. (5.108), for

We expect a similar relation for any surface C. In this generalisation, the 1-form λsing
depends on the g additional moduli from remark 5.8. This 1-form may for instance be

5.4
Liouville blocks through CFT-nonabelianisation
In the previous subsection, we successfully recovered Liouville conformal blocks from
degenerate FN-type networks using the free-field formalism, demonstrating how powerful
a tool this can be. However, there are two unresolved issues:
137


## Page 138


1. The free-field formalism is naturally defined for integer screening numbers, so
correlators with generic continuous momenta arise only after analytic continuation.
2. The free-field correlators do not capture the complete wall-crossing information that
we expect the 4d N = 2 partition function to encode.
Our analysis of the second problem indicates that a more systematic approach comes
from extending the free-field formalism to smooth spectral curves. As it turns out, such
an extension naturally resolves the need for analytic continuation and generalises the
free-field formalism to arbitrary surfaces. This was precisely the reasoning behind [35],
in the c = 1 setting, which we seek to generalise below to c ̸= 1. In the remainder of this
section, unless explicitly stated, we strictly assume that c ̸= 1, i.e. Q ̸= 0.
5.4.1
Reformulation of the traditional free-field formalism
In this section, we rephrase the traditional free-field formalism for Liouville theory
in terms of an abelianised boson eφ living on the singular cover Σsing. The fact that the
free-field formalism can be naturally formulated on the cover, follows in essence from
writing the Liouville boson φ as a linear combination
∂φ = 1
2 (∂φ1 −∂φ2) ,
(5.154)
where the fields φi are to be interpreted as the restriction of the abelianised boson eφ to the
ith sheet of the covering [34].

a local coordinate on U. Then, a free-field realisation of the Virasoro algebra Vir is defined

Thus, we are able to lift the traditional free-field formalism expressed in terms of the
Liouville boson φ on the base C = P1 to a theory of a non-local bosonic field eφ on a
degenerate cover Σsing. In order to extend the free-field formalism to smooth coverings Σ,
we shall have to treat the action of a branch-cut on eφ with more care.

Remark 5.20. So far, we have defined the free-field formalism on the spectral cover Σ.
However, from the above discussion it is also clear how to define free-field correlators
on the base C in terms of the Liouville boson φ. We would assign a single free-field
representation Vir(i) to all of C, and insert line defects supporting the Liouville reflection
operator RLiou along the branch-cuts on C. This operator relates the abelianisations of
the vertex operators Vα(z) and VQ−α(z) across branch-cuts, which are equivalent from
the perspective of Liouville theory. (In the semi-classical limit, the action of the reflection
operator simply corresponds to a permutation of the sections si in the local basis (s1, s2)
on either side of the branch-cut.)
Relating the singular and smooth abelianisations
Let us take one step back and take
stock of what we have done so far in section 5.4. We started in §5.4.1 with a reformulation
of the traditional free-field formalism for singular coverings Σsing →C. In particular, we
found (see eq. (5.168)) that primary vertex operators Vα(z) are abelianised as
Vab
α (z) = eα eφ(z(1)) ⊗e−α eφ(z(2)).
(5.204)
Instead, in §5.4.2 we argued that for smooth coverings Σ →C we need to dress the lifts of
branch-cuts to Σ with the R-matrix R1,2, and this lead (see eq. (5.183)) to the abelianisation
eVab
α (z) = eα eφ(z(1)) ⊗e(α−Q) eφ(z(2)).
(5.205)
Below, we reconcile the two formulations.

both sheets as the standard orientation, and to the altered radial orientation as the MO
orientation.

have to keep in mind though that conformal properties on each sheet are deformed in
the presence of a non-vanishing background charge on each sheet. For instance, conju-
gates (5.208) are now computed with respect to the background charge −Q on sheet 2
(versus Q on sheet 1).

Remark 5.21. In the traditional definition of the free-field formalism on the base C, in
contrast to double branch-points, simple branch-points lead to singularities due to the
non-trivial monodromy of the Liouville boson φ. It was shown in [35], for Q = 0, that
these singularities are resolved by inserting screening charges along the three trajectories
emitted by the simple branch-point. Our reinterpretation of the free-field formalism is
intrinsically defined on the spectral cover Σ, but can be formulated on the base C using
remark 5.20. We are optimistic that the conclusion of [35] still holds in this setting.
Notwithstanding this, inserting screening charges along the trajectories of a spectral
network is crucial in the global free-field formalism to ensure that Liouville-Verlinde loop
operators around branch-points on C are trivial. This is the quantum generalisation of the
nonabelian flatness constraints for the standard W-nonabelianisation method.

Degenerate vertex operators
Whereas generic Liouville vertex operators on the base C
are abelianised into pairs of (conjugate) Heisenberg vertex operators on the cover Σ, the
story for degenerate vertex operators is slightly different. We already saw a glimpse of
this at the end of §4.3, where we found in eq. (4.80) that (assuming the abelianisation
map (5.183) for generic vertex operators) the abelianisation of a degenerate Liouville
operator on C is given by a direct sum of two degenerate Heisenberg operators on either
sheet of Σ. We identified these two contributions with the two independent solutions to
the BPZ equation associated with the degenerate Liouville operator. Here, we will give an
alternative argument from the CFT abelianisation perspective.
First, note that while for generic momenta α the Fock spaces Fα and FQ−α are isomorphic
as Virasoro representations, this symmetry breaks down for degenerate momentum αp,q
(defined in eq. (3.35)). For instance, only α1,2 = −b/2 (and not Q −α1,2) satisfies the
algebraic null-vector decoupling constraint

the R-matrix R1,2 as well, is singular when acting on degenerate operators [102]. Conse-
quently, the abelianisation map (5.183) in the MO orientation is ill-defined for degenerate
operators. We are therefore forced to consider an alternative approach.

Remark 5.24. As we already saw in remark 4.8, generic vertex operators and degenerate
vertex operators correspond to inequivalent types of brane insertions in the parent 6d
theory. From the 6d construction, we also know that abelianised degenerate operators must
correspond to individual brane insertions on the sheets of Σ (in contrast to generic vertex
operators). The abelianisation map (5.226) for degenerate operators is thus consistent with
a realisation in string theory.

The second free-field solution to the BPZ equation can be obtained by acting on the
degenerate operator V1,2(z) with a screening charge Q+ [84, 16]. From the representation
theory of the quantum group Uq(sl2) (or equivalently, the Virasoro algebra), we conclude
that a degenerate operator V1,2(z) can at most support a single screening charge Q+ [16].
Together, V1,2 and Q+ · V1,2 span a 2-dimensional representation of Uq(sl2), which is
a q-deformation of the 2-dimensional representation of SL(2, C).67 Hence, V1,2(z) and
Q+ · V1,2(z) will indeed generate all solutions of the corresponding BPZ equation.

5.4.3
CFT-nonabelianisation
In §5.4.2 we proposed a global free-field representation of the Virasoro algebra with
respect to any smooth covering Σ →C in terms of a single (non-local) boson eφ and branch-
cut defects BR supporting the R-matrix R1,2. The branch-cut defects intertwine between
159


## Page 160


a theory with background charge Q and −Q on sheets 1 and 2 of Σ, respectively. In this
section, we conjecture the existence of a CFT-nonabelianisation map
ψCFT
W
: CBequiv
Heis (Σ) →CBLiou(C)
(5.260)
that allows us to build Liouville conformal blocks on the base C using the global free-
field construction on Σ. This construction is similar to the one proposed in [35], suitably
generalised to the case Q ̸= 0. Our expectations can then be summarised as follows:
1. We start with a spectral network W subordinate to a smooth covering Σ →C. We
also fix a trivialisation τ of the covering, but the final result is not dependent on this.
2. We construct a basis for the relevant space
CBequiv
Heis (Σ)
(5.261)
of SL(2, C)-equivariant Heisenberg blocks on Σ. This basis is essentially determined
by the global free-field representation for the particular space of Liouville blocks that
we want to reproduce. In particular, it includes the insertion of the Maulik-Okounkov
R-matrix along lifts of the branch-cuts to the cover Σ. (More details follow in the
subsection on ”Equivariant Heisenberg conformal blocks”.)
3. We insert into the SL(2, C)-equivariant Heisenberg blocks the exponentiated operator
E[W] := exp(QW)
(5.262)
of screening charges along the W-trajectories. Similar as in W-nonabelianisation,
we claim that inserting this operator will ensure that the resulting Liouville mon-
odromies around the branch-points on C are trivial. Moreover, it guarantees that
the construction reduces to the matrix model description in §5.2 in the limit where
the covering Σ →C degenerates to a singular covering. (More details follow in the
subsection on ”Introducing screening charges”. )
4. For spectral networks of FN-type, the resulting collection of Heisenberg conformal
blocks on Σ can be identified with the standard basis of Liouville conformal blocks
on C (introduced in §3.1).71 (More details follow in the subsection on ”Liouville
conformal blocks”.)
5. For spectral networks of FG-type, the construction provides a definition for the
”Goncharov-Shen blocks” proposed in [38]. (More remarks, including a comment on
compatibility with the exact WKB method, follow in the subsection on ”Exact WKB
and Goncharov-Shen blocks”.)

the latter can be identified with the Coulomb branch of the dual 4d N = 2 SU(2) gauge

is equal to the dimension of the space of Liouville internal momenta through the AGT
correspondence.73 Thus, we are able to match the moduli of equivariant Heisenberg blocks
with the 3g −3 + n internal momenta of Liouville blocks on C.

Introducing screening charges
Here we go back to the MO orientation. Since we would
like to interpret the equivariant Heisenberg blocks on the cover Σ as Virasoro blocks on
the base C, we will need to check that they do not detect branch-points on C. That is, we
need to make sure that the Liouville-Verlinde operator around a branch-point is trivial.
Recall that a Liouville-Verlinde operator is described by moving a degenerate insertion

To argue that the insertion of the operator E[W] is indeed necessary, recall that it

– see for instance the

ODE in any open neighbourhood on C (see §3.2.2), the Heisenberg blocks making up the
basis B(z) should generate the local sections in the exact WKB analysis of the ODE in such
an open neighbourhood. In particular, in the vicinity of a branch-point, we may infer the
behaviour of the degenerate operators eV(k)
1,2 on sheet k using the WKB analysis.
For instance, the exact WKB analysis implies that for any two points z and z′ in the
same connected component of C\W −{branch-cuts} the bases B(z) and B(z′) are related
by a diagonal SL(2, C)-transformation, whereas across a branch-cut on C the two basis
elements in B(z) are simply exchanged. Both of these statements naturally follow from the
global free-field construction. Across a critical trajectory of W, however, the bases B(z)
and B(z′) are supposed to be related by a non-trivial triangular SL(2, C)-transformation.
This is not possible without an additional operator insertion, but is true when we insert
the operator E[W] into the Heisenberg blocks.

is consistent with remark 5.7, where we showed that free-field correlators with additional
screening charges along FN-type contours obey the same Ward identities as Liouville
conformal blocks. Relatedly, Liouville-Verlinde operators around punctures and pants

Suppose that we want to reproduce a Liouville block F Liou
C,⃗α , defined as in eq. (3.22),
with respect to a given pants decomposition of C. Then we start by fixing an FN-type
spectral network WFN relative to the pants decomposition. This network is defined with
respect to a double covering Σ →C. We choose a trivialisation of this double covering,
and dress the branch-cuts of the covering with line defects BR. Similarly as in the context
of W-abelianisation, CFT-nonabelianisation with respect to FN-type spectral networks
commutes with the operation of gluing gauged 3-spheres. This implies that to verify
the above proposal, we only need to check it on the level of the gauged 3-sphere. We
leave an extensive analysis for future work and only make a few remarks here about our
expectations.
The simplest example to consider is the 3-punctured sphere. Since its spectral cover Σ is
a genus zero surface, and the expression (5.23) for a genus zero Heisenberg block does not
explicitly depend on the background charge Q(ez) (as long as the momentum conservation

expect that the resulting free-field integral reproduces the Liouville correlator

Indeed, one would not expect the singular matrix-model integral, where we do not

be symmetric under permuting the parameters αk, since this integral is computed with
respect to a degenerate FN spectral network that is not symmetric under all permutations
of the three punctures. Instead, the CFT-nonabelianisation map is defined with respect to
a spectral network that is symmetric under all permutations of the three punctures. The

Exact WKB and Goncharov-Shen blocks
If we apply the CFT-nonabelianisation map ψCFT
W
to a spectral network of FG-type, the resulting conformal block is defined with respect to
an ideal triangulation on C, and therefore a candidate for the proposed generalisation by
Goncharov and Shen of conformal blocks dual to ideal triangulations [38]. We emphasize
that these FG-type blocks obey slightly different Ward identities (at the next-to-leading
order near the punctures) than the standard Liouville blocks (see remark 5.7). Relatedly,
the action of the quantum FG coordinates XFG
γ
is compatible with the corresponding FG-
network, and in particular Verlinde operators evaluated around punctures and pants cycle
are no longer diagonal. Hence, there is no notion of a Liouville momentum.

Similar as in the semi-classical case, we expect that the expressions for Goncharov-Shen

Appendix
A
Nonabelianisation commutes with gluing for FN net-
works
We break the proof of the claim, that W-nonabelianisation for the resolutions W±
FN
commutes with dissection the surface C into pairs of pants, into two parts:
• In the first part of the proof, we show that the non-abelian monodromy around
any pants cycle a (as well as any small loop around a puncture) is simply the push-
forward of the abelian parallel transport along its direct lifts. In other words, we
168


## Page 169


show that all detour paths that contribute to the non-abelianisation map at finite η
“decouple” in the limit η ↓0. (This argument requires the WKB framing from §2.3.)
• In the second part of the proof, we restrict ourselves to the parallel transport inside
a single pair of pants. We argue that any contribution to the non-abelian parallel
transport matrices Pa/b/c from §2.3 can be matched with the abelian parallel transport
along a detour path present at finite η (for small enough η), whereas any detour
path that cannot be matched with a contribution to Pa/b/c corresponds to a trajectory
that traverses a pants-tube and therefore ”decouples” in the limit η ↓0. (Again, this
argument requires the WKB framing.)
First, we focus on a single pants cycle a (which may as well be a small loop a around a
puncture). As an illustrative example, consider the spectral network from fig. 3 at phase
ϑ = ϑFN + η, with η > 0. (The argument for ϑ = ϑFN −η is similar.) This network is
illustrated in fig. 67, with the pants curve a = [p] coloured red. The non-abelian parallel
transport along p receives contributions from direct lifts of p to the cover as well as from
detour paths associated with network trajectories that intersect p. We observe that there
are just four trajectories (highlighted in bright shades of blue in fig. 67) that cross the
pants cycle; two in each direction. (For the loop a there would only be two intersecting
trajectories.) At any finite value of η, these trajectories wind around the pants tube (or
loop around a puncture) a finite, say N, number of times before terminating on a puncture
at the opposite side of the pants cycle. In the limit η ↓0 the winding number N goes to
infinity.
Let us describe the relevant detour paths for the pants curve a in more detail (the
discussion for the loop a is similar but simpler.) Fix base-points at the four intersections of
the pants-tube-traversing trajectories with the path p. These are illustrated as blue dots in
fig. 67. Label the four detour paths associated with these base-points as δL/R,u/d, where
the superscript L or R refers to the left or right molecule from which the corresponding
trajectory originates, whereas the superscript u or d refers to whether the trajectory origi-
nates from the upper or lower branch-point in that molecule. Both detour paths δL start on
sheet 1 and end on sheet 2 of the cover, whereas both detour paths δR start on sheet 2 and
end on sheet 1. For convenience, we add this information as a subscript, so that the four
relevant detour paths are named
δR,d

Intuitively, the pants-transversing trajectories take infinitely long to reach the puncture on

Even though in the above we discussed the example of the 4-punctured sphere, the
local structure of the trajectories is the same for any pants cycle (as well as any small
loop around a puncture) with respect to any resolution of an FN network. Therefore, we
conclude that the non-abelian monodromy of any WKB-framed flat connection ∇around
any pants cycle (or any small loop around a puncture) is simply the push-forward of the
abelian parallel transport along its direct lifts.
In the second part, we restrict to a single pair of pants and distinguish the trajectories w
that originate from a branch-point inside this pair of pants from the trajectories w′ that
originate outside this pair of pants. In fig. 6 we have highlighted the former in bright blue
for the left pair of pants on the 4-punctured sphere, as an example. Consider the nonabelian
parallel transport ePa/b/c along any path epa,b,c in the path groupoid, when restricted to this
pair of pants.
Now, any intersection of the path epa/b/c with a trajectory w corresponds to a non-zero
detour contribution to ePa/b/c, while any intersection with a trajectory w′ leads to a detour
contribution that decouples in the limit η →0. The direct and detour contributions
associated with the trajectories w combine into a non-abelian parallel transport matrix of
the form PFN,±

(B.25)
Note that the absolute value bars around the Vandermonde determinant prevent the
appearance of additional phase factors when permuting the variables zs. The subset
{z1, . . . , zk} may therefore be replaced by any subset of k distinct variables zs.
Furhermore, after taking away the absolute value bars, the identity (B.25) still holds as
an integral over any twisted homology cycle Γ on Cn\{zs = 0, 1, zt} [99]. This is because it
arises as a solution to a differential Gauss-Manin system. In particular, the same identity
holds for the imaginary axis integral.
